1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	With LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
11	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
12	
13	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
14	\paragraph{70B evaluation.}
15	\label{sec:70b-results}
16	On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.
17	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
18	LoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset; Appendix~\ref{app:power-analysis}) DR 0.22\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 43.95, $+$0.15\,pp); CF drops from 3.50\% (zero-shot) to 0.18--1.33\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\%\,$\to$\,1.18\%; \S\ref{sec:composability}).
19	
20	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
21	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
22	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
23	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same token-level constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}---CFG-based JSON structure enforcement plus vocabulary membership masking---providing a direct head-to-head comparison: RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 3\% DR reduction, vs.\ S1's 94\%, confirming that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE (Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
24	Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints yield R-F1 19.95--41.16 on SciERC LoRA (vs.\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47) with 9.1--50.8\% parse failure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}); the model-dependent disparity confirms grammar enforcement provides a model-independent structural guarantee.
25	BoostCD's~\cite{boostcd2025} boosting is potentially integrable with OntoCoDec.
26	
```

[Director 指令] **R18 写作修复批次（不依赖实验结果，立即执行）**

### P1: Framing 修复

**W1: PICARD 技术区别**
在 §6 Related Work 加 2-3 句明确区分 OntoCoDec 与 PICARD：
"Unlike PICARD (Scholak et al., 2021), which prunes invalid beam hypotheses post-generation, OntoCoDec applies proactive token-level masking before sampling, preventing invalid tokens from entering the probability distribution. Moreover, PICARD operates on single-output SQL queries without entity registry state; OntoCoDec maintains a dynamic entity-to-type registry across multi-relation IE outputs, enabling cross-field type consistency enforcement that PICARD's stateless pruning cannot express."

**W7: Abstract 重写（减数字，ZS/FT 分开）**
当前 abstract ~200 词含 15+ 数字。重写原则：
- FT 核心结果一段（DR ≤0.09%, R-F1 43-46%, CF ≤1.4%）
- ZS 诊断价值一句（42-99% DR reduction, revealing dataset-dependent effectiveness）
- 移除 ZS/FT 交替出现的数字
- 总数字控制在 8-10 个
- 保留 "four models (7-70B)" 但改为 "three 8B-class models with preliminary 70B validation"

**W4: 70B headline 降格**
- Abstract: "four autoregressive LMs (7--70B)" → "three 8B-class models (Qwen3, Llama-3.1, Mistral) with preliminary 70B-class validation (Llama-3.3-70B AWQ)"
- §5.4 标题改为 "Preliminary 70B-Class Validation"

**W2: κ̄ p=0.138 显式标注**
- Figure 2 caption 加 "(p=0.138, not statistically significant; n=3 schemas)"
- 检查全文是否有任何残留的 predictive/explanatory 措辞并移除
- 考虑将 κ̄ 分析段落从主文移入 appendix，主文仅一句引用

### P2: 结构调整

**W8: GCD baseline 表移入主文**
- Appendix D Table 15 的核心行（GCD-equivalent grammar-only vs OntoCoDec S1）移入 §5 作为 inline comparison 或小表
- 如果空间不够，至少在 §5.4 加 "Table 15 (Appendix D)" 的显式交叉引用

**W6: ZS/FT 篇幅平衡**
- §5.2 violation landscape 压缩 0.2-0.3 页（合并重复描述）
- §5.3 cross-model 压缩 0.1-0.2 页
- 释放空间给 vLLM 实验结果（Agent 正在跑）

**W9: Proposition 1 finite entity set remark**
在 §2 或 §3 Proposition 1 后加：
"Remark: For finite entity type sets $|\mathcal{T}|$, the type-consistency language is regular (a DFA with $O(|\mathcal{T}|^n)$ states suffices); however, for realistic schemas this construction is engineering-intractable, motivating our runtime registry approach."

### Minor
- Table 1：加 \midrule 或 \cmidrule 分隔 ZS/FT 行组
- InstructUIE 引用：§6 加 "(Wang et al., 2023)" 的简要定位
- §6 如仍过短，从 Appendix B 再提一段

完成后编译检查，确认 8 页。

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	With LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
11	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
12	
13	% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200
14	\paragraph{70B evaluation.}
15	\label{sec:70b-results}
16	On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.
17	% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained
18	LoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset; Appendix~\ref{app:power-analysis}) DR 0.22\%\,$\to$\,0\% (R-F1 43.95, $+$0.15\,pp); CF drops from 3.50\% (zero-shot) to 0.18--1.33\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\%\,$\to$\,1.18\%; \S\ref{sec:composability}).
19	
20	% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc
21	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
22	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
23	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same token-level constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}---CFG-based JSON structure enforcement plus vocabulary membership masking---providing a direct head-to-head comparison: RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 3\% DR reduction, vs.\ S1's 94\%, confirming that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE (Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
24	Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only constraints yield 9.1--50.8\% parse failure and R-F1 19.95--41.16 vs.\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47 (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}).
25

_source line 4317 · 2026-05-22T12:57:10.173Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016b1ijmXng7V89CxMz3oyfN`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp, $<$0.5\% true-positive loss); residual CF ($\leq$1.4\% fine-tuned; up to 12\% zero-shot) represents \emph{violation conversion}---ontologically invalid triples (DR) are replaced by individually valid but type-inconsistent ones---resolved by beam search on SciERC (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
7	Zero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent effectiveness (42--99\% DR reduction, total violation reduction 31\%; \S\ref{sec:experiments}) and characterizing constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) as a descriptive correlate ($n{=}3$ schemas).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B zero-shot and LoRA in \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01X95US6GRp2bs8bxxmfzWS5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.
8	NeuroLogic Decoding~\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.
9	In code generation, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
12	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
13	GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, representing training-time schema internalization without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (E-F1${=}$9.17, R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.
14	
15	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
16	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives.
17	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.
18	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.
19	Extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.
20	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.\nNeuroLogic Decoding~\\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, representing training-time schema internalization without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (E-F1${=}$9.17, R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.\nExtraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.\n",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 20
    }
  },

1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,
3	%          mvp_phase0_violation_rate_docred, mvp_phase0_docred_schema
4	
5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}), near-eliminating DR (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%) with minimal overhead; and LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}), achieving zero total violations on SciERC under favorable conditions (1.54$\times$ latency).
8	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe to isolate constraint effects; R-F1 2--8 reflects absent task-specific training, not method failure.
9	The deployment-relevant evaluation is the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%), where constraints compose with parameter optimization without degrading performance.
10	
11	\paragraph{Setup.}
12	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
13	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
14	All constraints are defined from schema-level supervision, independently of the evaluation split.
15	The framework handles both sources without modification.
16	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
17	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).
18	Baseline DR violations are 15.37\% (SciERC) and 18.46\% (DocRED), comparable rates associated with constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\S\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
19	
```

55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	RV and ET reach $\leq$0.03\% for all models on both datasets.
57	DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings.
58	CF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\%; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%; poor entity typing limits DR reduction to 42\%); fine-tuning resolves both (\S\ref{sec:composability}).

      "content": "% Section 5 — Cross-Model Validation (compressed, 830→~400 words)\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          llama_docred_unconstrained, llama_docred_full_s1\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} replicates S1 with Llama-3.1-8B and Mistral-7B under identical prompts.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.4pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\textbf{R-F1} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-7}\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Model}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & $\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{SciERC}\n  & Qwen (U)    & 15.37 & 1.39  & 0.40 & 0.08 & 8.23 \\\\\n  & Qwen (S1)   &  0.93 & 0     & 0    & 9.90 & 8.36 \\\\\n  \\cmidrule(l){2-7}\n  & Llama (U)   & 13.34 & 0.55  & 0    & 0.24 & 2.68 \\\\\n  & Llama (S1)  &  0.08 & 0     & 0    & 6.56 & 2.54 \\\\\n  \\cmidrule(l){2-7}\n  & Mistral (U) & 56.94 & 0     & 1.50 & 56.87$^\\dagger$ & 3.66 \\\\\n  & Mistral (S1)& 13.40 & 0     & 0    & 1.27  & 3.67 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{DocRED}\n  & Qwen (U)    & 18.46 & 29.66 & 2.48 & 0.10 & 7.74 \\\\\n  & Qwen (S1)   & 10.21 &  0.03 & 0    & 2.57 & 8.23 \\\\\n  \\cmidrule(l){2-7}\n  & Llama (U)   & 26.07 & 16.03 & 0.13 & 0.17 & 5.98 \\\\\n  & Llama (S1)  &  2.55 &  0    & 0    & 12.02& 6.27 \\\\\n  \\cmidrule(l){2-7}\n  & Mistral (U) & 57.42 & 42.35 & 7.58 & 24.78 & 4.34 \\\\\n  & Mistral (S1)& 33.20 &  0    & 0    & 0.19  & 5.52 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Three-model zero-shot validation. U: unconstrained; S1: full two-layer.\nParse failures: Llama DocRED 9.1\\%; others $\\leq$1.1\\%.\n$^\\dagger$Mistral: poor zero-shot calibration causes pervasive type inconsistencies.\nF1 $\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\end{table}\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1, mistral_scierc, mistral_docred\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nRV and ET reach $\\leq$0.03\\% for all models on both datasets.\nDR drops 76--99\\% on SciERC and 42--90\\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings.\nCF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%; poor entity typing limits DR reduction to 42\\%); fine-tuning resolves both (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Post-hoc filtering comparison.}\nPost-hoc filtering achieves DR${=}$0\\% but with 15--74\\% rejection rates (DocRED Qwen: 48\\% discarded); under fine-tuning, both achieve comparable F1 (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\n",

```text
1	% Section 3 — Method (Draft)
2	% OntoCoDec: Type-Valid Information Extraction
3	% via Compiled Ontology Constraints
4	
5	\section{Method}
6	\label{sec:method}
7	
8	\input{fig1_framework}
9	
10	We present OntoCoDec, a framework that translates IE ontologies into token-level constraints enforced during autoregressive generation.
11	The design is guided by a decidability analysis of IE type constraints.
12	
13	\paragraph{Constraint decidability taxonomy.}
14	\label{sec:decidability}
15	We classify IE type constraints into three levels: (1)~\emph{context-free}: vocabulary membership and JSON structure; (2)~\emph{prefix-decidable with state}: domain/range type validity, determined at each type-bearing position given the decoded relation type and entity registry; (3)~\emph{non-local}: cross-entity type consistency, which depends on the full output.
16	Unlike code generation where types are statically declared, IE constraints fall into level~2: entity types are inferred from text, the registry is built dynamically, and constraint targets depend on the co-occurring relation.
17	OntoCoDec enforces levels 1--2 (grammar and semantic layers, respectively); level~3 is addressed by beam search or post-hoc resolution (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}).
18	
19	\smallskip
20	\noindent
21	Existing grammar-constrained decoding frameworks (e.g., XGrammar) enforce level~1 constraints---structural validity and vocabulary membership---but IE domain/range rules require cross-position entity-type state that context-free grammars cannot express.
22	We formalize this gap:
23	
24	\smallskip
25	\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
26	\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
27	
28	\smallskip
29	\noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
30	Consider $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
31	An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
32	A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint.
33	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).
34	For finite entity sets, the valid language is technically regular but the corresponding DFA requires $O(|\mathcal{E}|^{|\text{entities}|})$ states---exceeding $10^{17}$ for DocRED (7 entity types, 20+ entities per document)---making direct DFA construction infeasible and motivating the grammar-based approach (Appendix~\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\hfill$\square$
35	
36	\noindent\textbf{Corollary} (empirical enabling dependency: a constraint whose satisfaction is a prerequisite for evaluating subsequent constraints)\textbf{.} The empirical enabling dependency is necessary: the grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
37	\smallskip
38	
39	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
40	
41	
42	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
43	\label{sec:formulation}
44	
45	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
46	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
47	%
48	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
49	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
50	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
51	$}
52	\end{equation}
53	%
54	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
55	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
56	The grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
57	
58	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
59	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
60	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
61	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured when no fallback is triggered; domain-only filtering otherwise) strictly stronger than independent domain/range checks (which over-permit 33--37\% spurious pairs on SciERC/DocRED; \S\ref{sec:rectangularity}).
62	
63	
64	\subsection{Grammar Layer}
65	\label{sec:grammar}
66	
67	A JSON Schema encoding Eq.~\eqref{eq:output-schema} with \texttt{enum} constraints ($\mathcal{E}$, $\mathcal{R}$) is compiled into a pushdown automaton via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}.
68	At step $t$, grammar-valid tokens $G(t) \subseteq V$ pass; all others receive logits $-\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).
69	
70	\subsection{Semantic Layer}
71	\label{sec:semantic}
72	
73	The semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.
74	
75	\paragraph{Entity registry.}
76	\label{sec:entity-registry}
77	The semantic layer builds a registry $\mathcal{D}: \text{name} \to \text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\{n_i, \tau_i\}$ adds $(n_i, \tau_i)$ to $\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.
78	At each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\textsc{HeadType}, \textsc{TailType}).
79	\label{sec:position-detection}
80	
81	\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}
82	\label{sec:dynamic-masking}
83	At type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \subseteq V$.
84	At a \textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \in \mathcal{D}$ and $\mathcal{D}[h_j] \in \operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.
85	At a \textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\operatorname{range}(r_j, \tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \in \mathcal{D}$.
86	This sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\S\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.
87	
88	\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}
89	We use \emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \textsc{Sen}/\textsc{Sid}/\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
90	
91	\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}
92	\label{sec:interaction}
93	The final mask is $G(t) \cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\S\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the empirical enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.
94	
95	
96	\subsection{Decoding Strategies}
97	\label{sec:strategies}
98	
99	\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}
100	Both layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.
101	
102	\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\label{sec:s2}
103	A first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.
104	S1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.
105	
```

all others receive logits $-\\infty$, enforcing structural validity, vocabulary membership ($r_j \\in \\mathcal{R}$, $\\tau_i \\in \\mathcal{E}$), but not domain/range constraints (which depend on entity types within each triple).\n\n\\subsection{Semantic Layer}\n\\label{sec:semantic}\n\nThe semantic layer enforces domain/range constraints through a stateful logits processor maintaining an entity registry during generation.\n\n\\paragraph{Entity registry.}\n\\label{sec:entity-registry}\nThe semantic layer builds a registry $\\mathcal{D}: \\text{name} \\to \\text{type}$ incrementally during entity generation: each completed entry $\\{n_i, \\tau_i\\}$ adds $(n_i, \\tau_i)$ to $\\mathcal{D}$, which is consulted during relation generation for type lookups.\nAt each step $t$, the processor pattern-matches the decoded prefix to identify the current field position; semantic masks activate only at type-bearing positions (\\textsc{HeadType}, \\textsc{TailType}).\n\\label{sec:position-detection}\n\n\\paragraph{Dynamic masking.}\n\\label{sec:dynamic-masking}\nAt type-bearing positions, the processor computes a semantic mask $S(t) \\subseteq V$.\nAt a \\textsc{HeadType} position, if $h_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$ and $\\mathcal{D}[h_j] \\in \\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$, the mask allows only the registered type; otherwise it falls back to all domain-valid types $\\operatorname{dom}(r_j)$.\nAt a \\textsc{TailType} position, the mask restricts to $\\operatorname{range}(r_j, \\tau^h_j)$ conditioned on the already-determined head type, further narrowing to the registered type when $t_j \\in \\mathcal{D}$.\nThis sequential check achieves best-effort pair-based enforcement (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}), strictly tighter than independent domain/range checking; ``best-effort'' reflects the grammar-first fallback (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), which prioritizes JSON integrity over type validity when constraints conflict.\n\n\\paragraph{Tokenizer-agnostic type masking.}\nWe use \\emph{continuation masking} to handle context-dependent tokenizations and prefix-shared types (e.g., \\textsc{Sen}/\\textsc{Sid}/\\textsc{Side} in WikiEvents): a compile-time prefix map identifies valid continuations for each type name, disambiguating over multiple steps across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-semantic interaction.}\n\\label{sec:interaction}\nThe final mask is $G(t) \\cap S(t)$; when empty, grammar-first fallback applies (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}), embodying the empirical enabling dependency: semantic constraints reduce DR by only 24\\% without grammar-enforced vocabulary.\n\n\n\\subsection{Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:strategies}\n\n\\paragraph{S1 (joint extraction): Single-pass decoding.}\nBoth layers are active throughout generation; the entity registry populates during entity decoding and is immediately available for relation-level type checking.\n\n\\paragraph{S2 (two-pass pipeline): Two-pass decoding.}\\label{sec:s2}\nA first pass extracts entities; these compile an input-dependent grammar for the second pass.\nS1 achieves higher R-F1 at comparable DR (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}) and is the primary configuration.\n",

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}); at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), four models (7--70B), and three tokenizer families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned; \S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); zero-shot evaluation (7--70B) reveals DR drops 42--99\% across models and datasets, with 76--99\% on SciERC, but total violations (DR+CF) decrease by $\sim$31\%, reflecting conversion that fine-tuning resolves (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
22	
23	We make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:
24	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
25	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.
26	    \item A comprehensive evaluation across 3 IE datasets, 4 models (7--70B), and 2 tasks: under fine-tuning, DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% with CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an enabling dependency where on SciERC neither layer alone exceeds 24\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); event extraction on WikiEvents validates applicability beyond RE (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EBxQdQu8XudoDBezCMLBkT`</summary>

```text
1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	where $\wedge$ denotes element-wise AND over the vocabulary $V$; a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the empirical enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property enforced only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation, with DR (ontologically invalid type pair) more severe than CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency).
17	
18	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
19	\label{eq:tightness}
20	We define per-relation constraint tightness $\kappa(r) = 1 - |\sigma(r)|/|\mathcal{E}|^2$ and the schema-level mean $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$.
21	Across three schemas---WikiEvents ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.029$), SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$)---DR reduction ranges from 94--99\% (low $\bar{\kappa}$) to 42--90\% (high $\bar{\kappa}$); entity type confusability is a critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study},~\ref{app:tcr}).
22	
23	\paragraph{Greedy limitation and fallback cascade.}
24	\label{sec:greedy-limitation}
25	The semantic layer commits entity types greedily---an instance of Level~3 (non-local) constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability})---so CF violations arise when the same entity receives different types across triples; beam search mitigates this in favorable configurations (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
26	Grammar-first fallback triggers on 0.5--5.7\% of type-bearing positions under zero-shot decoding, correlating with $\bar{\kappa}$; fine-tuning virtually eliminates fallback ($\leq$\,0.11\%), transitioning the semantic layer from primary enforcer to safety net.
27	Causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs exclusively at fallback positions (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Non-rectangularity and independent enforcement.}
30	\label{sec:rectangularity}
31	Type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED rectangular); independent enforcement is a sound over-approximation admitting 33--37\% spurious pairs; fine-tuning eliminates all residual DR (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
32	
```

  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \\emph{constraint tightness} ($\\bar{\\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).\nThe two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with LoRA fine-tuning, \\textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $<$0.5\\% true-positive loss); residual CF ($\\leq$1.4\\% fine-tuned; up to 12\\% zero-shot) represents \\emph{violation conversion}---ontologically invalid triples (DR) are replaced by individually valid but type-inconsistent ones---resolved by beam search on SciERC (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nZero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent effectiveness (42--99\\% DR reduction, total violation reduction 31\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}) and characterizing constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) as a descriptive correlate ($n{=}3$ schemas).\nThe framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B zero-shot and LoRA in \\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nThe two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves full domain/range (DR) reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% with negligible recall cost ($<$0.5\\% true-positive loss); residual cross-field violations (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%) represent \\emph{violation conversion}, resolved by beam search on SciERC (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nZero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent DR reduction (42--99\\%) and characterizing constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) as a descriptive correlate ($n{=}3$ schemas).\nPreliminary 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.\n\\end{abstract}"
}

1. Primary Request and Intent:

   - **GCD equivalence Appendix + Error Analysis framework**: Add comparison table and placeholder subsection

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - OntoCoDec: Two-layer constrained decoding (grammar + semantic) for IE
   - Grammar layer: CFG-based JSON structure + vocabulary membership (equivalent to GCD/BoostCD)
   - Semantic layer: Prefix-decidable with state, runtime entity registry
   - DR (Domain/Range) violations vs CF (Cross-Field) violations
   - Violation conversion: DR enforcement converts to CF; fine-tuning resolves
   - Constraint tightness κ̄: descriptive metric (n=3 schemas), NOT predictive
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/appendix/limitations
   - Page budget is extremely tight - every addition requires equal compression
   - Error analysis: 514 FN under LoRA+S1 on SciERC, only 1 (0.2%) blocked by constraints
   - GCD/BoostCD equivalence: grammar-only ablation (Table 3 row 2) is functionally identical

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **abstract.tex** — JUST MODIFIED (W7+W4 partial):
     - Rewrote to reduce numbers from 15+ to 7 core numbers
     - Changed "four autoregressive LMs (7--70B)" to "three 8B-class models"
     - Separated ZS/FT: FT results in one paragraph, ZS diagnostic in one sentence
     - Added "Preliminary 70B-class validation" as separate sentence
     - Current content:
     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.
     A decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
     The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves full domain/range (DR) reduction alone.
     Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% with negligible recall cost ($<$0.5\% true-positive loss); residual cross-field violations (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%) represent \emph{violation conversion}, resolved by beam search on SciERC (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
     Zero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent DR reduction (42--99\%) and characterizing constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}$) as a descriptive correlate ($n{=}3$ schemas).
     Preliminary 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

   - **section5_runtime.tex** — Modified multiple times across tasks:
     - GCD appendix cross-ref shortened: "see Appendix...for a detailed comparison" → "(Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison})"

     \paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
     \label{sec:constrained-baselines}
     Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same token-level constraints as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}---CFG-based JSON structure enforcement plus vocabulary membership masking---providing a direct head-to-head comparison: RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 3\% DR reduction, vs.\ S1's 94\%, confirming that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE (Appendix~\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).
     Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only constraints yield 9.1--50.8\% parse failure and R-F1 19.95--41.16 vs.\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47 (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}).

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints.\nA decidability analysis yields a two-layer architecture: a \\emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \\emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.\nThe two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves full domain/range (DR) reduction alone.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\nComposed with LoRA fine-tuning on three 8B-class models across two RE datasets (up to 96 relation types), \\textsc{OntoCoDec} achieves DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% with negligible recall cost ($<$0.5\\% true-positive loss); residual cross-field violations (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%) represent \\emph{violation conversion}, resolved by beam search on SciERC (\\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\nZero-shot evaluation serves as a diagnostic probe, revealing dataset- and calibration-dependent DR reduction (42--99\\%) and characterizing constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}$) as a descriptive correlate ($n{=}3$ schemas).\nPreliminary 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}) and event extraction on WikiEvents (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}) confirm generalizability beyond the primary 8B evaluation.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/abstract.tex"
  },

        "content": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\nWith LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nTotal latency: 1.54$\\times$ (beam 1.32$\\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B evaluation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nOn Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset; Appendix~\\ref{app:power-analysis}) DR 0.22\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 43.95, $+$0.15\\,pp); CF drops from 3.50\\% (zero-shot) to 0.18--1.33\\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.18\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same token-level constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}---CFG-based JSON structure enforcement plus vocabulary membership masking---providing a direct head-to-head comparison: RV/ET$\\to$0\\% but only 3\\% DR reduction, vs.\\ S1's 94\\%, confirming that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE (Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nWithout JSON structural enforcement, trie-only constraints yield 9.1--50.8\\% parse failure and R-F1 19.95--41.16 vs.\\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47 (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",

Tot.\\ included).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.\nCF: same entity assigned conflicting types across triples.\nS1 = single-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:strategies}), S2 = two-pass (\\S\\ref{sec:s2}).\nGrammar-only represents the ceiling of grammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}: vocabulary membership is enforced but cross-field semantic constraints are not.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nS1 reduces DR from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\\,$\\to$\\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\%, reaching 0\\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nPost-hoc filtering discards up to 48\\% of predictions rather than correcting them (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).\nTotal type-violation burden (Tot.\\,=\\,DR+CF) decreases from 15.45\\% to 10.83\\% ($\\downarrow$30\\%), reflecting \\emph{violation conversion}: DR enforcement replaces ontologically invalid triples with individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF 0.08\\%\\,$\\to$\\,9.90\\%); under fine-tuning, Tot.\\ drops to 1.18\\% with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Layer contributions.}\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\nGrammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \\emph{empirical enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 and CF; we adopt S1.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_qwen_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval,\n%           w5_llama_docred_lora_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\n% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% with no significant $\\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\\% CIs $\\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\\% false positives).\nCF drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned; Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 43.60, 46.18; \\S\\ref{sec:beam-results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.56 & 0.08 & 40.89 & 82.35 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.22 & 40.79 & 81.87 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\shortstack[l]{Llama\\\\3.1-8B}}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  0.49 & 0.08 & 44.55 & 84.77 & --- \\\\\n  & & S1    &  0.00 & 0.26 & 44.70 & 84.71 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Fine-tuning composability: LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 on SciERC (551 docs) and DocRED (998 dev docs).\nF1\\,$\\times$100. No $\\Delta$R-F1 is significant (Bonferroni-corrected; details in text); all DR reductions are ($p{<}0.001$).\nE-F1 includes parse-failure documents (Qwen PF: 7--8\\%; Llama PF: 2--3\\%).}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (f) Downstream impact of DR elimination\n%  exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Downstream impact.}\n\\label{sec:downstream-impact}\nAmong constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\\% match gold labels (``lucky violations''; Appendix~\\ref{app:lucky-violations}), and error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\\%) was blocked by constraints---the remaining 99.8\\% reflect model limitations (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\n",

        "content": "% Appendix — Error Analysis and Case Studies\n\n\\section{Error Analysis and Case Studies}\n\\label{app:case-studies}\n\n\\definecolor{violation}{RGB}{220,50,50}\n\\definecolor{fixed}{RGB}{34,139,34}\n\\definecolor{cfwarn}{RGB}{200,120,0}\n\\newcommand{\\viol}[1]{\\textcolor{violation}{\\st{#1}}}\n\\newcommand{\\fixd}[1]{\\textcolor{fixed}{\\textbf{#1}}}\n\nWe present four representative cases illustrating constraint behavior across violation types. Cases 1--3 demonstrate OntoCoDec's corrective mechanisms; Case 4 illustrates a limitation.\n\n\\subsection{Domain-Range Correction}\n\\label{app:case-dr}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Input (SciERC, Qwen3-8B):} \\textit{``We present a novel model-based approach to recover the shapes and the poses of transparent objects from known motion.''}\n\nThe unconstrained model assigns \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} to ``shapes'' and ``poses,'' producing \\textsc{Feature-of}(\\textsc{Task}, \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm}) triples that violate domain/range constraints. The semantic layer redirects the tail type to \\textsc{Task}, yielding schema-compliant triples (Table~\\ref{tab:case1}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{OntoCoDec corrects three Feature-of DR violations. \\textcolor{violation}{Red}/\\ding{55} = violation; \\textcolor{fixed}{\\textbf{green/bold}}/\\ding{51} = corrected (shape markers provided for colorblind accessibility).}\n\\label{tab:case1}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.2cm}lll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Triple} & \\textbf{Head} & \\textbf{Tail} & \\textbf{DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{Unconstrained (3 violations)}} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFeature-of(recover, shapes) & Task & \\textcolor{violation}{OtherSciTerm} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nFeature-of(recover, poses) & Task & \\textcolor{violation}{OtherSciTerm} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nFeature-of(recover, transp.\\ obj.) & Task & \\textcolor{violation}{OtherSciTerm} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nUsed-for(model approach, recover) & Method & Task & \\ding{51} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{OntoCoDec (0 violations)}} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFeature-of(recover, shapes) & Task & \\textcolor{fixed}{Task} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\nFeature-of(recover, poses) & Task & \\textcolor{fixed}{Task} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\nUsed-for(model approach, recover) & Method & Task & \\ding{51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Dual-Layer Vocabulary and Type Correction}\n\\label{app:case-rv-dr}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Input (DocRED, Qwen3-8B):} \\textit{``Skai TV is a Greek free-to-air television network based in Piraeus. It is part of the Skai Group, one of the largest media groups in the country.''}\n\nThe unconstrained model generates natural-language relation names (e.g., ``country of origin'') instead of Wikidata P-codes, triggering RV violations. The grammar layer forces valid P-codes; the semantic layer ensures type compatibility (Table~\\ref{tab:case2}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Layer~1 eliminates RV violations; Layer~2 corrects DR types on DocRED.}\n\\label{tab:case2}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.5cm}p{1.8cm}p{1.2cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Unconstrained Output} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Type} \\\\\n\\midrule\nrel=``country of origin'' & Not in schema & RV \\\\\nrel=``headquarters location'' & Not in schema & RV \\\\\nrel=``part of'' & Not in schema & RV \\\\\nrel=``location of formation'' & Not in schema & RV \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{OntoCoDec Output} & & \\\\\n\\midrule\nrel=P495 (Skai TV, Greece) & ORG$\\to$LOC & \\ding{51} \\\\\nrel=P159 (Skai TV, Piraeus) & ORG$\\to$LOC & \\ding{51} \\\\\nrel=P127 (Skai TV, Skai Group) & ORG$\\to$ORG & \\ding{51} \\\\\nrel=P17 (Piraeus, Greece) & LOC$\\to$LOC & \\ding{51} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Scale Effect on Schema Compliance}\n\\label{app:case-scale}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Input (SciERC):} \\textit{``This paper investigates the utility of applying standard MT evaluation methods (BLEU, NIST, WER and PER) to building classifiers to predict semantic equivalence and entailment.''}\n\nBoth Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4) mistype ``MT evaluation methods'' as \\textsc{Method} (gold: \\textsc{Metric}), but the downstream constraint behavior differs (Table~\\ref{tab:case3}). The 70B model produces fewer violations (2 vs.\\ 4) and chooses a different relation (Part-of vs.\\ Hyponym-of), yet OntoCoDec corrects both.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{8B vs.\\ 70B on the same input. OntoCoDec corrects violations at both scales; CF arises when type corrections conflict with entity-list assignments.}\n\\label{tab:case3}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Relation} & \\textbf{Types} & \\textbf{DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{Qwen3-8B Unconstrained}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, BLEU) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Metric} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, NIST) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Metric} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, WER) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Metric} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, PER) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Metric} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{Qwen3-8B OntoCoDec}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, BLEU) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{fixed}{Meth} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\nHyponym-of(MT eval, NIST) & Meth$\\to$\\textcolor{fixed}{Meth} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textcolor{cfwarn}{CF: BLEU typed Metric in entity list, Method in relation}} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{Llama-3.1-70B Unconstrained}} \\\\\nPart-of(BLEU, MT eval) & Metric$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Meth} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\nPart-of(NIST, MT eval) & Metric$\\to$\\textcolor{violation}{Meth} & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\\textit{Llama-3.1-70B OntoCoDec}} \\\\\nPart-of(BLEU, MT eval) & Metric$\\to$\\textcolor{fixed}{Generic} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\nPart-of(NIST, MT eval) & Metric$\\to$\\textcolor{fixed}{Generic} & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Limitation: Trajectory Divergence under Constraints}\n\\label{app:case-cf}\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Input (SciERC, Mistral-7B):} \\textit{``Amorph recognizes NE items in two stages: dictionary lookup and rule application.''}\n\nThe gold annotation types ``Amorph'' as \\textsc{Method} and ``NE items'' as \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, with Used-for(Amorph, NE items). The unconstrained model mistypes ``Amorph'' but coincidentally produces the correct relation. Under OntoCoDec, modified token probabilities alter the generation trajectory entirely and produce a different (non-gold) relation (Table~\\ref{tab:case4}). This illustrates a fundamental property of autoregressive generation under modified distributions: constraint enforcement can change the entire output path, not just individual token choices.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Constrained decoding changes the generation trajectory. The unconstrained model coincidentally produced the gold triple despite type errors.}\n\\label{tab:case4}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.2cm}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Output} & \\textbf{Gold?} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Gold} & Used-for(Amorph\\textsuperscript{Meth}, NE items\\textsuperscript{OtherSci}) & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Unconstr.} & Used-for(Amorph\\textsuperscript{OtherSci}, NE items\\textsuperscript{Task}) & \\textcolor{fixed}{\\ding{51}} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{OntoCoDec} & Used-for(Amorph\\textsuperscript{OtherSci}, recognizes\\textsuperscript{Gen}) & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\n & Feature-of(NE items\\textsuperscript{Task}, in two stages\\textsuperscript{Gen}) & \\textcolor{violation}{\\ding{55}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Error Analysis: Constraint Impact on Prediction Quality}\n\\label{app:error-analysis}\n\n% exp_id: w5_lora_scierc_full_s1_eval (514 FN, 668 FP, 21 CF)\nWe analyze errors from Qwen3-8B LoRA + S1 on SciERC (551 documents; R-F1 43.77, 460 TP, 668 FP, 514 FN) across three categories.\n\n\\paragraph{False negatives: constraint-blocked vs.\\ model-limited.}\nOf 514 false negatives, only one (0.2\\%) is attributable to constraint enforcement:\n\n\\noindent\\textit{fn\\_001} (constraint-blocked): ``\\textit{Experiments on the TREC Blog track test set show that both groups of credibility indicators significantly improve retrieval effectiveness.}'' \\\\\nGold: Evaluate-for(retrieval effectiveness\\textsuperscript{Metric}, credibility indicators\\textsuperscript{OtherSci}).\nThe unconstrained model generates this triple correctly, but under S1, conflicting type assignments to `credibility indicators' (OtherScientificTerm in entity list vs.\\ Method in Evaluate-for's tail) trigger a CF violation, redirecting generation to a reversed Used-for triple.\n\n\\noindent\\textit{fn\\_004} (relation type confusion): ``\\textit{Amorph recognizes NE items in two stages: dictionary lookup and rule application.}'' \\\\\nGold: Part-of(dictionary lookup\\textsuperscript{Meth}, Amorph\\textsuperscript{Meth}).\nThe model predicts Used-for instead of Part-of---both are schema-valid for Method$\\to$Method pairs, so constraints provide no corrective signal. This Part-of$\\leftrightarrow$Used-for confusion is the dominant FN pattern.\n\n\\paragraph{False positives: schema-valid but incorrect.}\n\n\\noindent\\textit{fp\\_001} (wrong relation type): Same sentence as fn\\_004. The model generates Used-for(dictionary lookup, Amorph) where gold is Part-of. Used-for is the majority relation in SciERC (56\\% of all relations), causing systematic over-prediction. Since both relation types are schema-valid for the entity type pair, constraints cannot distinguish them.\n\n\\noindent\\textit{fp\\_007} (hallucinated relation): ``\\textit{The classifiers show little gain from information about meeting context.}'' \\\\\nNo gold relation exists. The model generates Used-for(meeting context\\textsuperscript{OtherSci}, classifiers\\textsuperscript{Meth}), ignoring the negation in ``show little gain from.'' The instrumental preposition ``from'' triggers relation generation despite the negative result.\n\n\\paragraph{CF violations: entity type polysemy.}\n\n\\noindent\\textit{cf\\_001} (Generic$\\leftrightarrow$Method): ``\\textit{A basic reason behind this is that the overall approach extends the approach described in a companion paper\\ldots}'' \\\\\nThe entity `approach' receives Generic in one Used-for relation and Method in another. The gold type is Generic; the polysemy of `approach' (generic reference vs.\\ specific method) causes per-relation type assignment to diverge.\n\n\\noindent\\textit{cf\\_005} (Material$\\leftrightarrow$Task): ``\\textit{Experimental evaluations on two challenging person re-identification datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed model\\ldots}'' \\\\\nThe compound noun `person re-identification datasets' contains both a task component and a data component. The model assigns Material in one Evaluate-for relation and Task in another within the same sentence.\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\nStructural constraints eliminate type-invalid outputs (DR$\\to$0.09\\%), but semantic ambiguity within schema-valid space---relation type confusion, entity type polysemy---remains the dominant error source. Only 1 of 514 false negatives (0.2\\%) was attributable to constraint enforcement, confirming that the recall cost of type constraints is negligible.\n",

        "totalLines": 178
      }

1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}); at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), four models (7--70B), and three tokenizer families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned; \S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); zero-shot evaluation (7--70B) reveals DR drops 42--99\% across models and datasets, with 76--99\% on SciERC, but total violations (DR+CF) decrease by $\sim$31\%, reflecting conversion that fine-tuning resolves (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
22	
23	We make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:
24	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
25	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.
26	    \item A comprehensive evaluation across 3 IE datasets, 4 models (7--70B), and 2 tasks: under fine-tuning, DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% with CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an enabling dependency where on SciERC neither layer alone exceeds 24\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); event extraction on WikiEvents validates applicability beyond RE (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MKausfb37tynSDxwth1oLU`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.
8	NeuroLogic Decoding~\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.
9	In code generation, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
10	
11	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
12	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
13	GoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, representing training-time schema internalization without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (E-F1${=}$9.17, R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.
14	
15	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
16	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives.
17	EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.
18	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.
19	Extraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.
20	
```
