  "uuid": "ec14938b-9144-4ff1-8c3c-e1c043cb649e",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_related_work.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.\nNeuroLogic Decoding~\\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, representing training-time schema internalization without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (E-F1${=}$9.17, R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.\nExtraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.\n",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 20
    }
  },

1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)
2	% exp_ids: mvp_phase0_violation_rate, mvp_phase0_scierc_schema,

5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}), near-eliminating DR (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%) with minimal overhead; and LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}), achieving zero total violations on SciERC under favorable conditions (1.54$\times$ latency).
8	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe to isolate constraint effects; R-F1 2--8 reflects absent task-specific training, not method failure.
9	The deployment-relevant evaluation is the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%), where constraints compose with parameter optimization without degrading performance.
10	
11	\paragraph{Setup.}
12	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
13	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
14	All constraints are defined from schema-level supervision, independently of the evaluation split.
15	The framework handles both sources without modification.
16	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
17	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).
18	Baseline DR violations are 15.37\% (SciERC) and 18.46\% (DocRED), comparable rates associated with constraint tightness rather than ontology scale (\S\ref{sec:introduction}); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
19	
```

4	\section{Extended Discussion}\label{app:discussion}
5	
6	%%% CF Dual Mechanism %%%
7	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1
8	
9	\paragraph{CF Dual Mechanism.}
10	CF violations arise from two distinct mechanisms:
11	(1)~\emph{Registry override}: the semantic constraint forces a domain-compatible type that conflicts with the entity's previously registered type.
12	The entity appears in multiple relations with different type requirements, and the per-relation mask selects the locally valid type without consulting prior assignments.
13	(2)~\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): when the semantic mask conflicts with all grammar-valid tokens, the semantic layer is dropped entirely, allowing the grammar layer to generate any structurally valid type, which may differ from the entity's registered type.
14	
15	These mechanisms have sharply different model profiles.
16	Qwen3-8B triggers zero grammar-first fallbacks across all experiments, attributing 100\% of its CF violations to registry override.
17	Llama-3.1-8B, by contrast, triggers 5{,}882 fallbacks on SciERC (10.7 per document) and 57{,}825 on DocRED (63.8 per document), making grammar-first fallback the dominant CF source.
18	This asymmetry is consistent with the cross-model CF gap: Qwen achieves CF\,=\,2.57\% on DocRED vs.\ Llama's 12.02\%, despite both using identical constraint configurations.
19	The difference traces to Llama's less calibrated type predictions, which more frequently conflict with the semantic mask's valid set, triggering fallback.
20	
21	%%% Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage %%%
22	% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (final data)
23	
24	\paragraph{Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage.}
25	\label{app:fallback-coverage}
26	The two-layer architecture produces two distinct fallback mechanisms with different semantic consequences.
27	\emph{Grammar-first fallback} occurs when $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$: the semantic layer deactivates as a safety valve, preserving structural validity at the cost of potential type violations.
28	The token-level fallback rate (36.6\% on SciERC Llama) is inflated by continuation tokens of multi-token type names (e.g., \textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 BPE tokens); at semantically meaningful first-token positions, the rate is 0.04--5.7\% (zero-shot) and drops to $\leq$\,0.11\% under fine-tuning.
29	These events account for the residual DR that persists after constraint enforcement.
30	\emph{Registry-consistent fallback} (4.5\%) occurs when the entity's registered type conflicts with domain/range constraints; the system defaults to the registered type for cross-triple consistency.
31	Together, these mechanisms produce the characteristic profile of composable constraint systems: residual DR traces to grammar-first fallback events, while CF violations arise from registry override events where per-relation enforcement assigns conflicting types.
32	
33	We define \emph{effective semantic constraint coverage} as $1 - f_{\text{gf}}$, where $f_{\text{gf}}$ is the grammar-first fallback rate.
34	On SciERC, Llama-3.1-8B shows $f_{\text{gf}} = 36.6\%$ (6{,}237 of 17{,}024 mask calls), yielding 63.4\% effective coverage.
35	On DocRED, by contrast, Qwen3-8B shows $f_{\text{gf}} = 0.08\%$ (46 of 54{,}859), yielding 99.9\% coverage.
36	The large discrepancy is primarily a tokenization artifact: SciERC entity types are multi-token (e.g., \textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 mask calls per type field), and continuation tokens necessarily conflict with the semantic mask, inflating the per-mask-call fallback rate.
37	DocRED types (\textsc{PER}, \textsc{ORG}, \textsc{LOC}, \textsc{TIME}, \textsc{NUM}, \textsc{MISC}) are all single-token, eliminating this inflation.
38	This metric directly quantifies the empirical enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): it measures the fraction of decoding steps where both constraint layers are jointly active, providing a fine-grained view beyond aggregate violation rates.
39	Qwen3-8B, with zero grammar-first fallbacks, achieves 100\% effective coverage on SciERC, consistent with its lower residual DR (0.93\% vs.\ Llama's 0.08\%).
40	
41	%%% Fallback Causal Tracing %%%
42	% exp_ids: Task C causal tracing (Qwen/Llama × ZS/FT × S1)
43	
44	\paragraph{Fallback Causal Tracing.}
45	\label{app:causal-tracing}
46	
47	To establish that grammar-first fallback is the \emph{sole} mechanism producing residual DR violations, we instrument the constraint processor to log, for each violation, whether the semantic mask was active ($S(t) \cap G(t) \neq \emptyset$) or bypassed via fallback ($S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$) at the type-bearing position.
48	Table~\ref{tab:causal-tracing} reports the results across four SciERC configurations.
49	
50	\begin{table}[t]
51	\centering
52	\small
53	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrr@{}}
54	\toprule
55	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Fallback Rate} & \textbf{DR} & \textbf{From FB} \\
56	\midrule
57	Qwen3-8B  & FT S1 & 0/2256 (0\%)    & 0  & N/A \\
58	Qwen3-8B  & ZS S1 & 16/3458 (0.46\%) & 16 & 100\% \\
59	Llama-3.1-8B & FT S1 & 1/2162 (0.05\%) & 1  & 100\% \\
60	Llama-3.1-8B & ZS S1 & 1/2630 (0.04\%) & 1  & 100\% \\
61	\bottomrule
62	\end{tabular}
63	\caption{Fallback causal tracing on SciERC (551 documents). Fallback Rate: type-bearing positions where $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ triggers grammar-first fallback. DR: residual domain/range violations. From FB: fraction of DR violations occurring at fallback positions. All violations occur exclusively at tail\_type positions (range constraints are tighter than domain constraints).}
64	\label{tab:causal-tracing}
65	\end{table}
66	
67	When the semantic mask is active, $\text{DR}_{\text{independent}} = 0$: the type constraint processor structurally prevents domain/range violations.
68	All 18 residual DR violations across four configurations occur exclusively at fallback positions, confirming that grammar-first fallback is a necessary condition for residual DR.
69	Fine-tuning reduces fallback to near-zero ($\leq$0.05\%), effectively eliminating the sole source of residual violations.
70	All fallback events occur at tail\_type positions, where $|\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)|$ is smaller than $|\operatorname{dom}(r)|$, making the intersection $S(t) \cap G(t)$ more likely to be empty.
71	
72	%%% CF-DR Trade-off Analysis %%%
73	% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (CF fallback vs full_s1 baseline)
74	
75	\paragraph{CF-DR Trade-off Analysis.}
76	\label{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}
77	
78	CF violations are the most prominent residual limitation of OntoCoDec under zero-shot decoding (9.90\% on SciERC, up to 12.02\% on Llama DocRED).
79	We treat this honestly: per-relation independent enforcement cannot eliminate CF without trading it against DR.
80	Table~\ref{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff} reports a pilot ablation in which we replace the grammar-first fallback with a CF-priority fallback that forces the entity's registered type when it conflicts with $\sigma(r)$.
81	
82	\begin{table}[t]
83	\centering
84	\small
85	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrr@{}}
86	\toprule
87	\textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{DR\,(\%)} & \textbf{CF\,(\%)} & \textbf{DR+CF} & \textbf{$f_{\text{gf}}$\,(\%)} \\
88	\midrule
89	S1 (baseline) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 10.83 & --- \\
90	CF fallback        & 14.78 & 0.04 & 14.82 & 36.64 \\
91	\midrule
92	$\Delta$ & $+$13.85 & $-$9.86 & $+$3.99 & --- \\
93	\bottomrule
94	\end{tabular}
95	\caption{CF-DR trade-off pilot on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents; \texttt{w1\_cf\_fallback\_scierc\_pilot}). CF fallback eliminates CF but re-routes violations to DR, increasing total violations (DR+CF) by 3.99\,pp. $f_{\text{gf}}$: token-level grammar-first fallback rate at type-bearing positions (includes continuation tokens of multi-token type names; first-token position fallback rate is 0.46\% for SciERC Qwen; \S\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}).}
96	\label{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff}
97	\end{table}
98	
99	The CF fallback variant nearly eliminates CF (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.04\%, $-$9.86\,pp) but at the cost of a 15.9$\times$ increase in DR (0.93\%\,$\to$\,14.78\%, $+$13.85\,pp).
100	% exp_id: w1_cf_fallback_scierc_pilot: DR=0.1478, CF=0.0004, f_gf=0.3664
101	This is not a net improvement: total violations (DR+CF) rise from 10.83\% to 14.82\% ($+$3.99\,pp), so the change \emph{re-routes} violations rather than reducing them.
102	The mechanism is mechanical: each token position with conflicting masks must commit to one violation type; when the CF fallback forces the system to emit the entity's registered type even though it lies outside $\sigma(r)$, the resulting triple satisfies cross-entity consistency but violates domain/range.
103	The token-level grammar-first fallback is triggered at 36.64\% of type-bearing positions (0.46\% at first-token positions; \S\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); this conflict is structural, not a tunable hyperparameter.
104	
105	We interpret this as a fundamental tension in composable constraint systems with \emph{per-relation independent} enforcement: enforcement priority can be allocated between DR and CF but cannot independently minimize both.
106	Within this design, the current S1 configuration sits near the Pareto frontier of the trade-off, achieving 0.93\% DR at the cost of 9.90\% CF.
107	The direction of the trade-off is consistent beyond SciERC/Qwen3-8B: across all six zero-shot configurations (3 models $\times$ 2 datasets), DR and CF are descriptively inversely associated, with no setting showing both high DR and high CF simultaneously (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model}); however, $n{=}6$ is insufficient for statistical inference on this correlation.
108	The pattern is a structural consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback, not a model- or dataset-specific artifact; we select SciERC/Qwen3-8B for detailed ablation because it exhibits the highest CF (9.90\%), making the trade-off most visible.
109	While the inverse association holds across all configurations, the absolute trade-off magnitude may differ in settings with higher baseline CF, such as Llama DocRED (CF = 12.02\%), where the larger CF headroom could yield proportionally larger shifts under alternative fallback priorities.
110	The CF residual is concentrated in schema ambiguity rather than enforcement failure: 63.3\% of CF violations on SciERC involve the catch-all \textsc{Generic} type (Table~\ref{tab:cf-distribution}), which competes with every content type depending on anaphoric vs.\ referential use, a distinction the per-slot decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.
111	
112	Two complementary paths can lower CF without re-routing it to DR.
113	(i)~\emph{Reduce the baseline conflict rate.} Composing OntoCoDec with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC reduces CF by 88\% relative to the zero-shot setting (9.90\%\,$\to$\,1.18\%; Table~\ref{tab:composability}), because the fine-tuned model's per-slot type predictions agree more often with the entity registry, so the per-relation mask and the registry intersect more frequently.
114	% exp_id: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval: CF=0.0118
115	The remaining 1.18\% still traces to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity, so this path is empirically validated on one dataset and one fine-tuning method but does not generalize without further evidence.
116	(ii)~\emph{Replace per-relation enforcement with global entity-level type consensus during decoding.} This is outside the scope of OntoCoDec's forward-only autoregressive design and is left to future work.
117	
118	%%% Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis %%%
119	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1,
120	%          llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1
121	
122	\paragraph{Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis.}
123	\label{app:dual-factor}
124	
125	Figure~\ref{fig:dual-factor-scatter} plots residual DR against the product $\bar{\kappa} \times \text{RV}_{\text{baseline}}$ for model$\times$dataset configurations.
126	For \emph{calibrated} models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B; unconstrained ET${<}$3\% on both datasets), a linear fit yields $y = 0.366x - 0.138$ with $R^2 = 0.886$ ($n{=}4$, $F(1,2) = 15.54$, $p \approx 0.058$); with only four data points (df $= 2$) and $p > 0.05$, this constitutes a descriptive characterization rather than a confirmatory statistical test.
127	The strong fit supports the dual-factor pattern from \S\ref{sec:composition}: residual DR is governed by the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate.
128	Mistral-7B (unconstrained ET${=}$7.58\% on DocRED) deviates from this pattern: its residual DR on DocRED (33.21\%) substantially exceeds the linear prediction. When model calibration is insufficient (ET${>}$5\% on either dataset), residual DR is dominated by entity misclassification rather than constraint-search failure.
129	This motivates a principled exclusion criterion: models with unconstrained ET${>}$5\% on either dataset fall outside the regime where the tightness$\times$RV product is the primary DR driver.
130	The two DocRED points (high $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) separate cleanly from the two SciERC points (low $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), and within each dataset the model with higher baseline RV shows higher residual DR.
131	
132	\begin{figure}[t]
133	\centering
134	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/dual_factor_scatter.pdf}
135	\caption{Residual DR vs.\ Schema Restrictiveness $\times$ Baseline Violation Rate ($\bar{\kappa} \times \text{RV}_{\text{baseline}}$) across four model$\times$dataset configurations ($n{=}3$ schemas; descriptive only).
136	Linear fit (calibrated models): $y = 0.366x - 0.138$, $R^2 = 0.886$; Mistral deviates due to entity misclassification.}
137	\label{fig:dual-factor-scatter}
138	\end{figure}
139	
140	%%% Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact (moved from §7) %%%
141	% exp_ids: d012_bootstrap_ci (DocRED +0.50pp p<0.001, SciERC +0.13pp p=0.58),
142	%          phase0_docred_unconstrained (RV=29.66%), phase0_scierc_unconstrained (RV=1.39%)
143	
144	\paragraph{Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact.}
145	The divergence in F1 significance between DocRED ($+$0.50\,pp, $p < 0.001$) and SciERC ($+$0.13\,pp, $p = 0.58$) traces to a single mechanism: grammar-layer elimination of out-of-vocabulary relation names.
146	DocRED's baseline RV of 29.66\% produces 5{,}189 OOV predictions counted as false positives; eliminating them yields a significant precision gain.
147	SciERC's baseline RV is only 1.39\% (24 OOV predictions), too few to produce a detectable F1 effect.
148	Llama-3.1-8B's lower baseline RV (16.03\%) yields a smaller but directionally consistent improvement ($+$0.29\,pp vs.\ $+$0.50\,pp); F1 gain magnitude is proportional to baseline OOV rate.
149	% exp_ids: phase0_docred_unconstrained, phase0_scierc_unconstrained
150	% exp_ids: llama_docred_unconstrained (RV=16.03%), llama_docred_full_s1 (R-F1 delta=+0.29pp)
151	
152	%%% Paragraph 1: Low F1 Practical Significance %%%
153	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%), phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%),
154	%          phase0_scierc_unconstrained (DR=15.37%), phase2_docred_unconstrained (DR=18.46%)
155	
156	\paragraph{Practical Significance of Zero-Shot F1.}
157	Our reported relation F1 of approximately 8\% on both SciERC and DocRED reflects the inherent difficulty of zero-shot closed IE, where the model receives no task-specific training examples.
158	For reference, supervised state-of-the-art systems on SciERC achieve approximately 49--53\% relation F1 with full training data~\citep{zhong2021frustratingly,ye2022packed}.
159	Constraint enforcement operates largely independently of the extraction model's intrinsic capability: it restricts the output space to ontologically valid triples regardless of the underlying model's accuracy.
160	Even after LoRA fine-tuning, residual DR of 1--2\% persists on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); constraints remain necessary at deployment-grade quality.
161	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval
162	In our evaluation, F1 serves as a \emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.
163	The contribution of this work is the constraint enforcement method itself; the zero-shot setting stress-tests constraint robustness under maximum model uncertainty, while composability with fine-tuning (\S\ref{sec:composability}) validates practical deployment value.
164	
165	%%% Paragraph 4: Decoding-Time vs. Post-Hoc Enforcement %%%
166	% exp_ids: posthoc_filtering_baseline, phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%),
167	%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%, ET=0%)
168	
169	\paragraph{Decoding-Time vs.\ Post-Hoc Enforcement.}
170	Post-hoc filtering (generating triples without constraints, then removing schema-violating predictions) yields higher precision and consequently higher F1 than OntoCoDec on both datasets (SciERC 8.63 vs.\ 8.36; DocRED 9.61 vs.\ 8.23).
171	% posthoc: SciERC F1=8.63, DocRED F1=9.61; OntoCoDec: SciERC R-F1=8.36, DocRED R-F1=8.23
172	However, this precision gain comes at substantial cost.
173	On DocRED, post-hoc filtering discards 48.1\% of all predicted triples, including 127 true positives (11.2\% of correct predictions); recall drops from 9.46\% to 8.40\%.
174	% posthoc_docred: rejection=48.1%, tp_lost=127/1137, R: 9.46→8.40
175	Entity type violations remain unresolved (ET\,=\,2.47\% vs.\ OntoCoDec's 0\%), as filtering targets only domain/range and vocabulary violations.
176	% posthoc_docred: ET_residual=2.47%; phase2_docred_full_s1: ET=0%
177	OntoCoDec's decoding-time approach avoids this information loss by guiding generation toward compliant outputs rather than discarding non-compliant ones, preserving both recall and entity type validity.
178	The two strategies are complementary: post-hoc filtering can serve as an additional safety net for residual violations that decoding-time enforcement does not fully resolve.
179	
180	%%% CF Violation Distribution %%%
181	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (CF=245), llama_scierc_full_s1 (CF=162),
182	%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (CF=2670), llama_docred_full_s1 (CF=3824)

184	\paragraph{Cross-Field Violation Distribution.}
185	\label{app:cf-distribution}
186	
187	Table~\ref{tab:cf-distribution} reports the complete distribution of conflicting-type pairs for SciERC S1 (245 CF violations, all pairwise; no mention triggered $\geq$3 types).
188	Conflicts involving the \textsc{Generic} type account for 63.3\% (155/245) of all violations.
189	\textsc{Generic} is SciERC's anaphoric/placeholder label (``this approach'', ``the method''), which competes with every content type depending on whether the mention is used anaphorically or referentially, a distinction the per-slot constrained decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.
190	
191	The pattern generalizes across models and datasets: on Llama-3.1-8B SciERC, \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} remains the top pair (53/162, 32.7\%); on DocRED, the analogous catch-all type \textsc{MISC} drives 76.6\% of Qwen's CF violations.
192	Under unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\leq$1\% on both datasets; CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.

225	Table~\ref{tab:lora-per-relation} presents the per-relation breakdown of DR elimination when OntoCoDec is composed with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC.
226	Fine-tuning alone eliminates DR on four of seven relations but leaves non-zero violations on the three tightest-constraint relations: \textsc{Evaluate-for} ($\kappa{=}0.361$, 13.13\%), \textsc{Part-of} ($\kappa{=}0.417$, 5.77\%), and \textsc{Compare} ($\kappa{=}0.528$, 5.00\%).
227	Adding OntoCoDec constraints eliminates all remaining violations across all relations.
228	One \textsc{Evaluate-for} triple (99\,$\to$\,98) is redirected to \textsc{Used-for} (634\,$\to$\,635) by token-level masking: the head entity's registered type falls outside \textsc{Evaluate-for}'s domain, and the semantic mask steers generation to the nearest valid relation.

293	  & & Grammar       & 0.51 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.12 & 2.30 & 84.04 & 43.77 \\

299	DR/RV/ET/CF: violation rates (\%); PF: parse failure rate (\%); F1 $\times$100.
300	Grammar layer is a no-op post-fine-tuning (DR change $<$0.2\,pp in all 4 settings; RV and ET already 0\% without constraints).
301	Semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense, providing 39--69\% reduction.
302	F1 cost is negligible: max $\Delta$R-F1 = $-$0.10\,pp (Qwen DocRED, full vs.\ unconstrained).}

305	
306	The grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\,pp (4/4 settings).
307	The semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 39--69\% across all settings (SciERC: Qwen $-$69\%, Llama $-$41\%; DocRED: Qwen $-$63\%, Llama $-$39\%).
308	Adding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.
309	F1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across S1 vs.\ unconstrained is $-$0.10\,pp (Qwen DocRED).
310	
311	%%% Residual DR Error Analysis (Fine-Tuned) %%%
312	% exp_ids: w5_qwen_docred_*, w5_llama_docred_*, w5_lora_scierc_*, w5_llama_lora_scierc_*
313	
314	\paragraph{Residual DR Error Analysis.}
315	\label{app:residual-dr-analysis}
316	
317	Across all fine-tuned settings (2 models $\times$ 2 datasets $\times$ S1), only 36 domain/range violations remain.
318	Manual inspection reveals four root causes:
319	
320	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
321	\item \emph{Entity type errors} (39\%): the LLM assigns an incorrect entity type (e.g., labeling a geographic entity as \textsc{LOC} when the gold standard requires \textsc{MISC}), causing the semantic mask to permit a domain/range-incompatible relation.
322	\item \emph{Schema coverage gaps} (33\%): the frequency-based constraint table, constructed from training-set co-occurrence statistics, does not cover long-tail type pairs that are ontologically valid but rare. These violations are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone.
323	\item \emph{Incorrect relation selection} (17\%): the model selects a semantically inappropriate relation whose domain/range constraints are incompatible with the correctly typed entities.
324	\item \emph{Direction reversal} (11\%): the model assigns the correct relation but inverts head/tail roles, placing entities in positions that violate the relation's asymmetric domain/range specification.
325	\end{enumerate}
326	
327	Two dataset-specific patterns emerge.
328	Outside Llama DocRED, all residual violations (3 of 36, spanning Llama SciERC and Qwen DocRED) trace exclusively to schema coverage gaps: the model's entity typing is accurate, and the constraint system has reached its theoretical ceiling given the frequency-based constraint table.
329	On DocRED, Llama-3.1-8B contributes 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), with entity type errors as the dominant cause (14/33, 42\%).
330	This asymmetry reflects Llama's weaker fine-tuned entity typing on DocRED's larger ontology (96 relations, 7 entity types) compared to Qwen, which achieves near-zero residual.
331	The implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), so the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.
332

377	%%% Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling %%%
378	
379	\paragraph{Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling.}
380	\label{app:runtime}
381	
382	On SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot greedy, NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition), unconstrained inference averages 4.163\,s/doc and S1 constrained decoding averages 4.710\,s/doc ($+$13.1\% overhead).
383	On DocRED (same configuration), relative overhead is lower due to amortization: the fixed per-token constraint cost is spread over longer generation sequences.
384	XGrammar automaton compilation takes 0.044\,s; the full constraint pipeline (schema parsing, JSON Schema generation, grammar compilation) takes 0.33\,s on SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:scalability}), amortized to zero over the evaluation set.
385	
386	% exp_id: p8c_ft_scierc_latency — Reviewer Report #23 verified
387	\paragraph{Fine-Tuned Latency Profiling.}
388	\label{app:ft-latency}
389	Under LoRA fine-tuning (same hardware and dataset), unconstrained inference averages 2.612\,s/doc and S1 constrained decoding averages 2.873\,s/doc ($+$10.0\% overhead, $n{=}551$).
390	Fine-tuned models generate shorter, more structured outputs than zero-shot models (2.612\,s vs.\ 4.163\,s/doc unconstrained); absolute generation time drops by 37\%.
391	The relative overhead ($+$10.0\%) is slightly lower than zero-shot ($+$13.1\%), consistent with shorter sequences requiring fewer constraint checks per document.
392	
393	%%% Fuzzy Relation Matching %%%
394	
395	\paragraph{Relation Matching Protocol.}
396	\label{app:fuzzy-matching}
397	Relation F1 evaluation uses fuzzy normalization to handle model-specific formatting variants (e.g., underscores vs.\ spaces, abbreviations).
398	A predicted relation $r_p$ matches gold relation $r_g$ if: (1)~exact string match after case-insensitive normalization, or (2)~token overlap $>$80\% \emph{and} edit distance $\leq 2$.
399	This protocol affects primarily Mistral, which produces underscore-separated variants (e.g., ``head\_of\_government'' for ``head of government'').
400	On Mistral DocRED, fuzzy matching contributes 98 additional TPs ($+$0.84\,pp R-F1; strict R-F1\,=\,3.50\% vs.\ fuzzy 4.34\%).
401	For Qwen and Llama under grammar constraints, the grammar layer enforces exact relation names, making fuzzy matching irrelevant (0 additional matches).
402	All F1 figures in the main text use fuzzy normalization consistently across all models and conditions.
403

438	%%% WikiEvents Extended %%%
439	
440	\paragraph{WikiEvents Dataset Details.}
441	\label{app:wikievents}
442	WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} is a document-level event argument extraction benchmark grounded in the DARPA KAIROS ontology.
443	It defines 56 event types, 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\_type, role)$\to$allowed\_entity\_types constraints.
444	The grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the semantic layer enforces AC (argument constraint) via role-conditioned masking, analogous to RE domain/range enforcement.
445	
446	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness in WikiEvents.}
447	\label{app:wikievents-kappa}
448	% Data source: artifacts/w1c_wikievents_kappa_results.md
449	WikiEvents role-level tightness (with $|\mathcal{E}|{=}27$) has mean $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.82$ and median 0.89; 84\% of roles have $\kappa \geq 0.80$ (4 or fewer valid entity types out of 27).
450	The tightness-violation pattern extends to event extraction: roles with $\kappa \geq 0.90$ exhibit the highest AC rate (50\%), compared to 21\% for medium-tightness ($0.80 \leq \kappa < 0.90$) and 30\% for loose ($\kappa < 0.80$) roles; this is consistent with the RE observation that constraint tightness correlates with violation frequency.
451	However, the correlation is modulated by entity type prototypicality: semantically intuitive roles (e.g., Transportation.Vehicle$\to$VEH) show 0\% AC despite $\kappa{=}0.963$, while roles that require rare KAIROS-specific types (e.g., Crime$\to$CRM, Sentence$\to$SEN) show near-100\% AC because LLMs default to common types (PER, ORG).
452	The loose group's elevated rate (30\%) is driven by high-frequency roles such as Attack.Target (126 args, 34\% AC, $\kappa{=}0.59$), where systematic model errors outweigh the permissive constraint space.
453	
454	%%% Lucky Violation Analysis %%%
455	% exp_ids: task_e_lucky_violation_analysis

457	\paragraph{Lucky Violation Analysis.}
458	\label{app:lucky-violations}
459	Table~\ref{tab:lucky-violations} quantifies the recall cost of DR elimination.
460	A ``lucky violation'' is a constraint-removed triple that happens to match a gold label---an ontologically invalid prediction that is nonetheless correct.
461	Across four fine-tuned configurations, lucky violations average 0.49\% of gold triples (range 0.01--0.51\%), confirming that nearly all removed DR violations are false positives.
462	On Llama SciERC, all five lucky violations are recovered as legal type paths under constrained decoding (zero churn): the model finds an ontology-compliant route to the same prediction.

490	
491	\paragraph{Trie-Only Baseline (GCD-Equivalent).}
492	\label{app:trie-baseline}
493	Table~\ref{tab:trie-baseline} isolates the effect of vocabulary membership constraints without JSON structural enforcement.
494	The trie constrains entity type and relation tokens to schema-valid values (achieving ET${=}$0\%, RV${=}$0\% across all configurations), functionally equivalent to GCD's~\cite{geng2023gcd} constrained decoding.
495	However, without grammar-level JSON enforcement, models systematically omit entity type fields (83\% of triples) or produce malformed JSON (0--51\% parse failure), causing DR to rise to 15--96\%---far worse than unconstrained baselines.
496	The effect is most dramatic for Llama-8B LoRA: 50.8\% parse failure causes R-F1 to collapse from 45.47 (OntoCoDec S1) to 19.95, demonstrating that the grammar layer is not merely helpful but a structural prerequisite---vocabulary constraints require JSON scaffolding to reach the positions where they apply.

535	\paragraph{Comparison with Grammar-Based Constrained Decoding.}
536	\label{app:gcd-comparison}
537	
538	Table~\ref{tab:gcd-comparison} summarizes the relationship between OntoCoDec's constraint layers and existing grammar-based constrained decoding methods.
539

550	Trie-Only (Tab.~\ref{tab:trie-baseline}) & Prefix trie & Vocab & 0\%$^\ddagger$ & N/A & Stateless \\

557	$^\dagger$Equivalent mechanism; BoostCD adds beam-level reranking but the CFG enforcement is identical.
558	$^\ddagger$Vocabulary-only constraints without JSON structure cause DR to \emph{increase} (15--96\%; Table~\ref{tab:trie-baseline}).}
559	\label{tab:gcd-comparison}
560	\end{table}
561	
562	Our grammar layer implements the same CFG-based token masking as GCD and BoostCD---the mechanisms are formally identical (JSON schema $\to$ pushdown automaton $\to$ per-step allowed token set).
563	Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} row~2 therefore constitutes a direct comparison: grammar-only enforcement achieves RV/ET$\to$0\% but only ${\sim}$3\% DR reduction, identical to what GCD-family methods would achieve on IE tasks.
564	The critical gap these methods cannot address is cross-field type consistency (Level~2), which requires the stateful semantic layer that maintains an entity registry across relation slots and enforces per-relation domain/range constraints at each type-bearing position.
565

mechanisms:\n(1)~\\emph{Registry override}: the semantic constraint forces a domain-compatible type that conflicts with the entity's previously registered type.\nThe entity appears in multiple 100\\% of its CF violations to registry override.\nLlama-3.1-8B, by contrast, triggers 5{,}882 fallbacks on SciERC (10.7 per document) and 57{,}825 on DocRED (63.8 per document), making grammar-first fallback the dominant CF source.\nThis asymmetry is consistent with the cross-model CF gap: Qwen achieves CF\\,=\\,2.57\\% on DocRED vs.\\ Llama's 12.02\\%, despite both using identical constraint configurations.\nThe difference traces to Llama's less calibrated type predictions, which more frequently conflict with the semantic mask's valid set, triggering fallback.\n\n%%% Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage %%%\n% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (final data)\n\n\\paragraph{Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage.}\n\\label{app:fallback-coverage}\nThe two-layer architecture produces two distinct fallback mechanisms with different semantic consequences.\n\\emph{Grammar-first fallback} occurs when $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$: the semantic layer deactivates as a safety valve, preserving structural validity at the cost of potential type violations.\nThe token-level fallback rate (36.6\\% on SciERC Llama) is inflated by continuation tokens of multi-token type names (e.g., \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 BPE tokens); at semantically meaningful first-token positions, the rate is 0.04--5.7\\% (zero-shot) and drops to $\\leq$\\,0.11\\% under fine-tuning.\nThese events account for the residual DR that persists after constraint enforcement.\n\\emph{Registry-consistent fallback} (4.5\\%) occurs when the entity's registered type conflicts with domain/range constraints; the system defaults to the registered type for cross-triple consistency.\nTogether, these mechanisms produce the characteristic profile of composable constraint systems: residual DR traces to grammar-first fallback events, while CF violations arise from registry override events where per-relation enforcement assigns conflicting types.\n\nWe define \\emph{effective semantic constraint coverage} as $1 - f_{\\text{gf}}$, where $f_{\\text{gf}}$ is the grammar-first fallback rate.\nOn SciERC, Llama-3.1-8B shows $f_{\\text{gf}} = 36.6\\%$ (6{,}237 of 17{,}024 mask calls), yielding 63.4\\% effective coverage.\nOn DocRED, by contrast, Qwen3-8B shows $f_{\\text{gf}} = 0.08\\%$ (46 of 54{,}859), yielding 99.9\\% coverage.\nThe large discrepancy is primarily a tokenization artifact: SciERC entity types are multi-token (e.g., \\textsc{OtherScientificTerm}, averaging 2.87 mask calls per type field), and continuation tokens necessarily conflict with the semantic mask, inflating the per-mask-call fallback rate.\nDocRED types (\\textsc{PER}, \\textsc{ORG}, \\textsc{LOC}, \\textsc{TIME}, \\textsc{NUM}, \\textsc{MISC}) are all single-token, eliminating this inflation.\nThis metric directly quantifies the empirical enabling dependency (\\S\\ref{sec:interaction}): it measures the fraction of decoding steps where both constraint layers are jointly active, providing a fine-grained view beyond aggregate violation rates.\nQwen3-8B, with zero grammar-first fallbacks, achieves 100\\% effective coverage on SciERC, consistent with its lower residual DR (0.93\\% vs.\\ Llama's 0.08\\%).\n\n%%% Fallback Causal Tracing %%%\n% exp_ids: Task C causal tracing (Qwen/Llama × ZS/FT × S1)\n\n\\paragraph{Fallback Causal Tracing.}\n\\label{app:causal-tracing}\n\nTo establish that grammar-first fallback is the \\emph{sole} mechanism producing residual DR violations, we instrument the constraint processor to log, for each violation, whether the semantic mask was active ($S(t) \\cap G(t) \\neq \\emptyset$) or bypassed via fallback ($S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$) at the type-bearing position.\nTable~\\ref{tab:causal-tracing} reports the results across four SciERC configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Fallback Rate} & \\textbf{DR} & \\textbf{From FB} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-8B  & FT S1 & 0/2256 (0\\%)    & 0  & N/A \\\\\nQwen3-8B  & ZS S1 & 16/3458 (0.46\\%) & 16 & 100\\% \\\\\nLlama-3.1-8B & FT S1 & 1/2162 (0.05\\%) & 1  & 100\\% \\\\\nLlama-3.1-8B & ZS S1 & 1/2630 (0.04\\%) & 1  & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Fallback causal tracing on SciERC (551 documents). Fallback Rate: type-bearing positions where $S(t) \\cap G(t) = \\emptyset$ triggers grammar-first fallback. DR: residual domain/range violations. From FB: fraction of DR violations occurring at fallback positions. All violations occur exclusively at tail\\_type positions (range constraints are tighter than domain constraints).}\n\\label{tab:causal-tracing}\n\\end{table}\n\nWhen the semantic mask is active, $\\text{DR}_{\\text{independent}} = 0$: the type constraint processor structurally prevents domain/range violations.\nAll 18 residual DR violations across four configurations occur exclusively at fallback positions, confirming that grammar-first fallback is a necessary condition for residual DR.\nFine-tuning reduces fallback to near-zero ($\\leq$0.05\\%), effectively eliminating the sole source of residual violations.\nAll fallback events occur at tail\\_type positions, where $|\\operatorname{range}(r, \\tau^h)|$ is smaller than $|\\operatorname{dom}(r)|$, making the intersection $S(t) \\cap G(t)$ more likely to be empty.\n\n%%% CF-DR Trade-off Analysis %%%\n% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (CF fallback vs full_s1 baseline)\n\n\\paragraph{CF-DR Trade-off Analysis.}\n\\label{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}\n\nCF violations are the most prominent residual limitation of OntoCoDec under zero-shot decoding (9.90\\% on SciERC, up to 12.02\\% on Llama DocRED).\nWe treat this honestly: per-relation independent enforcement cannot eliminate CF without trading it against DR.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff} reports a pilot ablation in which we replace the grammar-first fallback with a CF-priority fallback that forces the entity's registered type when it conflicts with $\\sigma(r)$.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Configuration} & \\textbf{DR\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{CF\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{DR+CF} & \\textbf{$f_{\\text{gf}}$\\,(\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nS1 (baseline) & 0.93 & 9.90 & 10.83 & --- \\\\\nCF fallback        & 14.78 & 0.04 & 14.82 & 36.64 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & $+$13.85 & $-$9.86 & $+$3.99 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{CF-DR trade-off pilot on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents; \\texttt{w1\\_cf\\_fallback\\_scierc\\_pilot}). CF fallback eliminates CF but re-routes violations to DR, increasing total violations (DR+CF) by 3.99\\,pp. $f_{\\text{gf}}$: token-level grammar-first fallback rate at type-bearing positions (includes continuation tokens of multi-token type names; first-token position fallback rate is 0.46\\% for SciERC Qwen; \\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}).}\n\\label{tab:cf-dr-tradeoff}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe CF fallback variant nearly eliminates CF (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.04\\%, $-$9.86\\,pp) but at the cost of a 15.9$\\times$ increase in DR (0.93\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.78\\%, $+$13.85\\,pp).\n% exp_id: w1_cf_fallback_scierc_pilot: DR=0.1478, CF=0.0004, f_gf=0.3664\nThis is not a net improvement: total violations (DR+CF) rise from 10.83\\% to 14.82\\% ($+$3.99\\,pp), so the change \\emph{re-routes} violations rather than reducing them.\nThe mechanism is mechanical: each token position with conflicting masks must commit to one violation type; when the CF fallback forces the system to emit the entity's registered type even though it lies outside $\\sigma(r)$, the resulting triple satisfies cross-entity consistency but violates domain/range.\nThe token-level grammar-first fallback is triggered at 36.64\\% of type-bearing positions (0.46\\% at first-token positions; \\S\\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); this conflict is structural, not a tunable hyperparameter.\n\nWe interpret this as a fundamental tension in composable constraint systems with \\emph{per-relation independent} enforcement: enforcement priority can be allocated between DR and CF but cannot independently minimize both.\nWithin this design, the current S1 configuration sits near the Pareto frontier of the trade-off, achieving 0.93\\% DR at the cost of 9.90\\% CF.\nThe direction of the trade-off is consistent beyond SciERC/Qwen3-8B: across all six zero-shot configurations (3 models $\\times$ 2 datasets), DR and CF are descriptively inversely associated, with no setting showing both high DR and high CF simultaneously (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}); however, $n{=}6$ is insufficient for statistical inference on this correlation.\nThe pattern is a structural consequence of per-relation enforcement with grammar-first fallback, not a model- or dataset-specific artifact; we select SciERC/Qwen3-8B for detailed ablation because it exhibits the highest CF (9.90\\%), making the trade-off most visible.\nWhile the inverse association holds across all configurations, the absolute trade-off magnitude may differ in settings with higher baseline CF, such as Llama DocRED (CF = 12.02\\%), where the larger CF headroom could yield proportionally larger shifts under alternative fallback priorities.\nThe CF residual is concentrated in schema ambiguity rather than enforcement failure: 63.3\\% of CF violations on SciERC involve the catch-all \\textsc{Generic} type (Table~\\ref{tab:cf-distribution}), which competes with every content type depending on anaphoric vs.\\ referential use, a distinction the per-slot decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.\n\nTwo complementary paths can lower CF without re-routing it to DR.\n(i)~\\emph{Reduce the baseline conflict rate.} Composing OntoCoDec with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC reduces CF by 88\\% relative to the zero-shot setting (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.18\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:composability}), because the fine-tuned model's per-slot type predictions agree more often with the entity registry, so the per-relation mask and the registry intersect more frequently.\n% exp_id: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval: CF=0.0118\nThe remaining 1.18\\% still traces to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity, so this path is empirically validated on one dataset and one fine-tuning method but does not generalize without further evidence.\n(ii)~\\emph{Replace per-relation enforcement with global entity-level type consensus during decoding.} This is outside the scope of OntoCoDec's forward-only autoregressive design and is left to future work.\n\n%%% Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1,\n%          llama_scierc_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1\n\n\\paragraph{Dual-Factor Quantitative Analysis.}\n\\label{app:dual-factor}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dual-factor-scatter} plots residual DR against the product $\\bar{\\kappa} \\times \\text{RV}_{\\text{baseline}}$ for model$\\times$dataset configurations.\nFor \\emph{calibrated} models (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B; unconstrained ET${<}$3\\% on both datasets), a linear fit yields $y = 0.366x - 0.138$ with $R^2 = 0.886$ ($n{=}4$, $F(1,2) = 15.54$, $p \\approx 0.058$); with only four data points (df $= 2$) and $p > 0.05$, this constitutes a descriptive characterization rather than a confirmatory statistical test.\nThe strong fit supports the dual-factor pattern from \\S\\ref{sec:composition}: residual DR is governed by the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate.\nMistral-7B (unconstrained ET${=}$7.58\\% on DocRED) deviates from this pattern: its residual DR on DocRED (33.21\\%) substantially exceeds the linear prediction. When model calibration is insufficient (ET${>}$5\\% on either dataset), residual DR is dominated by entity misclassification rather than constraint-search failure.\nThis motivates a principled exclusion criterion: models with unconstrained ET${>}$5\\% on either dataset fall outside the regime where the tightness$\\times$RV product is the primary DR driver.\nThe two DocRED points (high $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$) separate cleanly from the two SciERC points (low $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.333$), and within each dataset the model with higher baseline RV shows higher residual DR.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/dual_factor_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Residual DR vs.\\ Schema Restrictiveness $\\times$ Baseline Violation Rate ($\\bar{\\kappa} \\times \\text{RV}_{\\text{baseline}}$) across four model$\\times$dataset configurations ($n{=}3$ schemas; descriptive only).\nLinear fit (calibrated models): $y = 0.366x - 0.138$, $R^2 = 0.886$; Mistral deviates due to entity misclassification.}\n\\label{fig:dual-factor-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%%% Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact (moved from §7) %%%\n% exp_ids: d012_bootstrap_ci (DocRED +0.50pp p<0.001, SciERC +0.13pp p=0.58),\n%          phase0_docred_unconstrained (RV=29.66%), phase0_scierc_unconstrained (RV=1.39%)\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset-Dependent F1 Impact.}\nThe divergence in F1 significance between DocRED ($+$0.50\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) and SciERC ($+$0.13\\,pp, $p = 0.58$) traces to a single mechanism: grammar-layer elimination of out-of-vocabulary relation names.\nDocRED's baseline RV of 29.66\\% produces 5{,}189 OOV predictions counted as false positives; eliminating them yields a significant precision gain.\nSciERC's baseline RV is only 1.39\\% (24 OOV predictions), too few to produce a detectable F1 effect.\nLlama-3.1-8B's lower baseline RV (16.03\\%) yields a smaller but directionally consistent improvement ($+$0.29\\,pp vs.\\ $+$0.50\\,pp); F1 gain magnitude is proportional to baseline OOV rate.\n% exp_ids: phase0_docred_unconstrained, phase0_scierc_unconstrained\n% exp_ids: llama_docred_unconstrained (RV=16.03%), llama_docred_full_s1 (R-F1 delta=+0.29pp)\n\n%%% Paragraph 1: Low F1 Practical Significance %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%), phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%),\n%          phase0_scierc_unconstrained (DR=15.37%), phase2_docred_unconstrained (DR=18.46%)\n\n\\paragraph{Practical Significance of Zero-Shot F1.}\nOur reported relation F1 of approximately 8\\% on both SciERC and DocRED reflects the inherent difficulty of zero-shot closed IE, where the model receives no task-specific training examples.\nFor reference, supervised state-of-the-art systems on SciERC achieve approximately 49--53\\% relation F1 with full training data~\\citep{zhong2021frustratingly,ye2022packed}.\nConstraint enforcement operates largely independently of the extraction model's intrinsic capability: it restricts the output space to ontologically valid triples regardless of the underlying model's accuracy.\nEven after LoRA fine-tuning, residual DR of 1--2\\% persists on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}); constraints remain necessary at deployment-grade quality.\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval\nIn our evaluation, F1 serves as a \\emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.\nThe contribution of this work is the constraint enforcement method itself; the zero-shot setting stress-tests constraint robustness under maximum model uncertainty, while composability with fine-tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:composability}) validates practical deployment value.\n\n%%% Paragraph 4: Decoding-Time vs. Post-Hoc Enforcement %%%\n% exp_ids: posthoc_filtering_baseline, phase2_scierc_full_s1 (R-F1=8.36%),\n%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (R-F1=8.23%, ET=0%)\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding-Time vs.\\ Post-Hoc Enforcement.}\nPost-hoc filtering (generating triples without constraints, then removing schema-violating predictions) yields higher precision and consequently higher F1 than OntoCoDec on both datasets (SciERC 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23).\n% posthoc: SciERC F1=8.63, DocRED F1=9.61; OntoCoDec: SciERC R-F1=8.36, DocRED R-F1=8.23\nHowever, this precision gain comes at substantial cost.\nOn DocRED, post-hoc filtering discards 48.1\\% of all predicted triples, including 127 true positives (11.2\\% of correct predictions); recall drops from 9.46\\% to 8.40\\%.\n% posthoc_docred: rejection=48.1%, tp_lost=127/1137, R: 9.46→8.40\nEntity type violations remain unresolved (ET\\,=\\,2.47\\% vs.\\ OntoCoDec's 0\\%), as filtering targets only domain/range and vocabulary violations.\n% posthoc_docred: ET_residual=2.47%; phase2_docred_full_s1: ET=0%\nOntoCoDec's decoding-time approach avoids this information loss by guiding generation toward compliant outputs rather than discarding non-compliant ones, preserving both recall and entity type validity.\nThe two strategies are complementary: post-hoc filtering can serve as an additional safety net for residual violations that decoding-time enforcement does not fully resolve.\n\n%%% CF Violation Distribution %%%\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1 (CF=245), llama_scierc_full_s1 (CF=162),\n%          phase2_docred_full_s1 (CF=2670), llama_docred_full_s1 (CF=3824)\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Field Violation Distribution.}\n\\label{app:cf-distribution}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cf-distribution} reports the complete distribution of conflicting-type pairs for SciERC S1 (245 CF violations, all pairwise; no mention triggered $\\geq$3 types).\nConflicts involving the \\textsc{Generic} type account for 63.3\\% (155/245) of all violations.\n\\textsc{Generic} is SciERC's anaphoric/placeholder label (``this approach'', ``the method''), which competes with every content type depending on whether the mention is used anaphorically or referentially, a distinction the per-slot constrained decoder cannot resolve without document-level coreference.\n\nThe pattern generalizes across models and datasets: on Llama-3.1-8B SciERC, \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$\\textsc{OtherScientificTerm} remains the top pair (53/162, 32.7\\%); on DocRED, the analogous catch-all type \\textsc{MISC} drives 76.6\\% of Qwen's CF violations.\nUnder unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\\leq$1\\% on both datasets; CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Conflicting Type Pair} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGeneric $\\leftrightarrow$ OtherScientificTerm & 85 & 34.7 \\\\\nGeneric $\\leftrightarrow$ Material & 35 & 14.3 \\\\\nGeneric $\\leftrightarrow$ Metric & 30 & 12.2 \\\\\nMethod $\\leftrightarrow$ Task & 20 & 8.2 \\\\\nMaterial $\\leftrightarrow$ Method & 17 & 6.9 \\\\\nOtherScientificTerm $\\leftrightarrow$ Task & 12 & 4.9 \\\\\n\\midrule\n10 further pairs (each $\\leq$3.3\\%) & 46 & 18.8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Total} & \\textbf{245} & 100.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{CF violation distribution on SciERC S1 (Qwen3-8B, 551 documents).\nAll 245 violations are pairwise conflicts.\nConflicts involving \\textsc{Generic} account for 63.3\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cf-distribution}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning %%%\n% exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Relation DR Elimination under Fine-Tuning.}\n\\label{app:lora-per-relation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:lora-per-relation} presents the per-relation breakdown of DR elimination when OntoCoDec is composed with LoRA fine-tuning on SciERC.\nFine-tuning alone eliminates DR on four of seven relations but leaves non-zero violations on the three tightest-constraint relations: \\textsc{Evaluate-for} ($\\kappa{=}0.361$, 13.13\\%), \\textsc{Part-of} ($\\kappa{=}0.417$, 5.77\\%), and \\textsc{Compare} ($\\kappa{=}0.528$, 5.00\\%).\nAdding OntoCoDec constraints eliminates all remaining violations across all relations.\nOne \\textsc{Evaluate-for} triple (99\\,$\\to$\\,98) is redirected to \\textsc{Used-for} (634\\,$\\to$\\,635) by token-level masking: the head entity's registered type falls outside \\textsc{Evaluate-for}'s domain, and the semantic mask steers generation to the nearest valid relation.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l r rr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{LoRA Unconstr.}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{LoRA + S1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(l){5-6}\n\\textbf{Relation} & $\\kappa$ & DR\\,(\\%) & $n$ & DR\\,(\\%) & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nEvaluate-for & 0.361 & 13.13 & 99 & \\textbf{0} & 98 \\\\\nPart-of      & 0.417 & 5.77  & 52 & \\textbf{0} & 52 \\\\\nCompare      & 0.528 & 5.00  & 40 & \\textbf{0} & 40 \\\\\nConjunction  & 0.306 & 1.31  & 153 & \\textbf{0} & 153 \\\\\nFeature-of   & 0.278 & 1.35  & 74 & \\textbf{0} & 74 \\\\\nUsed-for     & 0.028 & 0.16  & 634 & \\textbf{0} & 635 \\\\\nHyponym-of   & 0.417 & 0     & --- & \\textbf{0} & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Per-relation DR elimination on SciERC with LoRA fine-tuning (Qwen3-8B).\n$\\kappa$: constraint tightness; $n$: predicted triple count.\nUsed-for gains one triple from Evaluate-for redirection.}\n\\label{tab:lora-per-relation}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Fine-Tuned Four-Way Ablation %%%\n% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_*, w5_llama_lora_scierc_*, w5_qwen_docred_*, w5_llama_docred_*, w8_*\n\n\\paragraph{Fine-Tuned Four-Way Ablation.}\n\\label{app:ft-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ft-ablation} presents the four-way constraint ablation on fine-tuned models across both datasets, isolating each layer's contribution when the base model has already internalized schema knowledge.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{RV\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{ET\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF} & \\textbf{PF} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{8}{*}{SciERC}\n  & \\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n    & Unconstrained & 1.95 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 67.05 & 43.86 \\\\\n  & & Grammar       & 1.90 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 67.05 & 43.86 \\\\\n  & & Semantic      & 0.60 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.18 & 0.00 & 67.05 & 43.69 \\\\\n  & & S1       & \\textbf{0}    & 0.00 & 0.00 & 1.18 & 0.00 & 67.05 & 43.77 \\\\\n  \\cmidrule(l){2-10}\n  & \\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n    & Unconstrained & 1.11 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 67.76 & 45.45 \\\\\n  & & Grammar       & 1.11 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 67.76 & 45.45 \\\\\n  & & Semantic      & 0.65 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.10 & 0.00 & 67.76 & 45.47 \\\\\n  & & S1       & \\textbf{0.09} & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.46 & 0.00 & 67.76 & 45.47 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{8}{*}{DocRED}\n  & \\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n    & Unconstrained & 0.56 & 0.27 & 0.00 & 0.08 & 7.11 & 82.35 & 40.89 \\\\\n  & & Grammar       & 0.61 & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.11 & 7.01 & 82.19 & 40.85 \\\\\n  & & Semantic      & 0.21 & 0.23 & 0.00 & 0.27 & 7.52 & 82.15 & 40.89 \\\\\n  & & S1       & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.22 & 8.52 & 81.87 & 40.79 \\\\\n 43.77 \\\\\n  & & Semantic      & 0.30 & 0.15 & 0.00 & 0.16 & 2.40 & 84.02 & 43.90 \\\\\n  & & S1       & \\textbf{0.00} & 0.00 & 0.00 & 0.26 & 2.51 & 84.71 & 44.70 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Four-way constraint ablation on fine-tuned models (LoRA, 2 models $\\times$ 2 datasets).\nDR/RV/ET/CF: violation rates (\\%); PF: parse failure rate (\\%); F1 $\\times$100.\nGrammar layer is a no-op post-fine-tuning (DR change $<$0.2\\,pp in all 4 settings; RV and ET already 0\\% without constraints).\nSemantic layer is the sole effective DR defense, providing 39--69\\% reduction.\nF1 cost is negligible: max $\\Delta$R-F1 = $-$0.10\\,pp (Qwen DocRED, full vs.\\ unconstrained).}\n\\label{tab:ft-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe grammar layer is effectively a no-op for fine-tuned models across all four settings: RV and ET are already 0\\% without constraints, and grammar-only DR change is $<$0.2\\,pp (4/4 settings).\nThe semantic layer is the sole effective DR defense under fine-tuning, independently reducing DR by 39--69\\% across all settings (SciERC: Qwen $-$69\\%, Llama $-$41\\%; DocRED: Qwen $-$63\\%, Llama $-$39\\%).\nAdding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% in all 4 settings): even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.\nF1 cost is negligible: the maximum R-F1 decrease across S1 vs.\\ unconstrained is $-$0.10\\,pp (Qwen DocRED).\n\n%%% Residual DR Error Analysis (Fine-Tuned) %%%\n% exp_ids: w5_qwen_docred_*, w5_llama_docred_*, w5_lora_scierc_*, w5_llama_lora_scierc_*\n\n\\paragraph{Residual DR Error Analysis.}\n\\label{app:residual-dr-analysis}\n\nAcross all fine-tuned settings (2 models $\\times$ 2 datasets $\\times$ S1), only 36 domain/range violations remain.\nManual inspection reveals four root causes:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Entity type errors} (39\\%): the LLM assigns an incorrect entity type (e.g., labeling a geographic entity as \\textsc{LOC} when the gold standard requires \\textsc{MISC}), causing the semantic mask to permit a domain/range-incompatible relation.\n\\item \\emph{Schema coverage gaps} (33\\%): the frequency-based constraint table, constructed from training-set co-occurrence statistics, does not cover long-tail type pairs that are ontologically valid but rare. These violations are irreducible by decoding-time constraints alone.\n\\item \\emph{Incorrect relation selection} (17\\%): the model selects a semantically inappropriate relation whose domain/range constraints are incompatible with the correctly typed entities.\n\\item \\emph{Direction reversal} (11\\%): the model assigns the correct relation but inverts head/tail roles, placing entities in positions that violate the relation's asymmetric domain/range specification.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nTwo dataset-specific patterns emerge.\nOutside Llama DocRED, all residual violations (3 of 36, spanning Llama SciERC and Qwen DocRED) trace exclusively to schema coverage gaps: the model's entity typing is accurate, and the constraint system has reached its theoretical ceiling given the frequency-based constraint table.\nOn DocRED, Llama-3.1-8B contributes 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), with entity type errors as the dominant cause (14/33, 42\\%).\nThis asymmetry reflects Llama's weaker fine-tuned entity typing on DocRED's larger ontology (96 relations, 7 entity types) compared to Qwen, which achieves near-zero residual.\nThe implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), so the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.\n\n%%% Post-Hoc Filtering Comparison (Extended) %%%\n% exp_ids: posthoc_*_zs, posthoc_*_ft\n\n\\paragraph{Post-Hoc Filtering: Extended Comparison.}\n\\label{app:posthoc-extended}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:posthoc-extended} presents the full comparison across all six zero-shot and four fine-tuned configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrr l@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Reject\\%} & \\textbf{R-F1$_{\\text{ph}}$} & \\textbf{R-F1$_{\\text{cd}}$} & \\textbf{Winner} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{ZS}\n  & Qwen    & SciERC & 16.8 & 8.63  & 8.36  & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & SciERC & 13.9 & 2.71  & 2.54  & ph \\\\\n  & Mistral & SciERC & 20.3 & 3.94  & 3.67  & ph \\\\\n  & Qwen    & DocRED & 48.1 & 9.61  & 8.23  & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & DocRED & 42.2 & 7.44  & 6.27  & ph \\\\\n  & Mistral & DocRED & \\textbf{73.7} & 3.85  & \\textbf{5.52} & cd \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{FT}\n  & Qwen    & SciERC & 1.95 & 43.94 & 43.77 & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & SciERC & 1.11 & 45.23 & 45.47 & cd \\\\\n  & Qwen    & DocRED & 1.03 & 41.11 & 40.79 & ph \\\\\n  & Llama   & DocRED & 0.64 & 43.92 & 44.70 & cd \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Post-hoc filtering (ph) vs.\\ constrained decoding (cd).\nIn zero-shot settings, post-hoc F1 is higher in 5/6 groups (precision-dominated), but constrained decoding preserves higher recall in all 6/6 groups.\nAt reject rate $>$70\\% (Mistral DocRED), post-hoc F1 collapses below constrained.\nUnder fine-tuning, rejection rates drop to 0.6--2.0\\% and the two strategies are equivalent ($\\Delta \\leq 0.55$\\,pp; each wins two groups).\nF1 $\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:posthoc-extended}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe crossover point occurs when post-hoc rejection exceeds $\\sim$70\\%: Mistral DocRED rejects 73.7\\% of predicted triples, discarding enough true positives that constrained decoding's recall advantage dominates ($+$1.67\\,pp).\nOntoCoDec's advantage thus scales with violation severity: precisely when constraints are most needed, post-hoc filtering is most destructive.\nUnder fine-tuning, both strategies converge because few violations remain to filter or constrain ($\\Delta \\leq 0.55$\\,pp across all four FT groups, with each strategy winning two).\n\n%%% Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-Shot Runtime Profiling.}\n\\label{app:runtime}\n\nOn SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot greedy, NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition), unconstrained inference averages 4.163\\,s/doc and S1 constrained decoding averages 4.710\\,s/doc ($+$13.1\\% overhead).\nOn DocRED (same configuration), relative overhead is lower due to amortization: the fixed per-token constraint cost is spread over longer generation sequences.\nXGrammar automaton compilation takes 0.044\\,s; the full constraint pipeline (schema parsing, JSON Schema generation, grammar compilation) takes 0.33\\,s on SciERC (Table~\\ref{tab:scalability}), amortized to zero over the evaluation set.\n\n% exp_id: p8c_ft_scierc_latency — Reviewer Report #23 verified\n\\paragraph{Fine-Tuned Latency Profiling.}\n\\label{app:ft-latency}\nUnder LoRA fine-tuning (same hardware and dataset), unconstrained inference averages 2.612\\,s/doc and S1 constrained decoding averages 2.873\\,s/doc ($+$10.0\\% overhead, $n{=}551$).\nFine-tuned models generate shorter, more structured outputs than zero-shot models (2.612\\,s vs.\\ 4.163\\,s/doc unconstrained); absolute generation time drops by 37\\%.\nThe relative overhead ($+$10.0\\%) is slightly lower than zero-shot ($+$13.1\\%), consistent with shorter sequences requiring fewer constraint checks per document.\n\n%%% Fuzzy Relation Matching %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Relation Matching Protocol.}\n\\label{app:fuzzy-matching}\nRelation F1 evaluation uses fuzzy normalization to handle model-specific formatting variants (e.g., underscores vs.\\ spaces, abbreviations).\nA predicted relation $r_p$ matches gold relation $r_g$ if: (1)~exact string match after case-insensitive normalization, or (2)~token overlap $>$80\\% \\emph{and} edit distance $\\leq 2$.\nThis protocol affects primarily Mistral, which produces underscore-separated variants (e.g., ``head\\_of\\_government'' for ``head of government'').\nOn Mistral DocRED, fuzzy matching contributes 98 additional TPs ($+$0.84\\,pp R-F1; strict R-F1\\,=\\,3.50\\% vs.\\ fuzzy 4.34\\%).\nFor Qwen and Llama under grammar constraints, the grammar layer enforces exact relation names, making fuzzy matching irrelevant (0 additional matches).\nAll F1 figures in the main text use fuzzy normalization consistently across all models and conditions.\n\n%%% Per-Relation Violation Landscape %%%\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Relation Violation Landscape.}\n\\label{app:per-relation-landscape}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:prompt_comparison} compares violation rates under unconstrained and schema-informed prompting.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per_relation_dr} shows the per-relation DR distribution on SciERC.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under unconstrained and schema-informed prompting (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).}\n\\label{tab:prompt_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & Prompt & DR$\\downarrow$ & RV$\\downarrow$ & ET$\\downarrow$ & CF$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{SciERC} & Unconstrained & 15.4 & 1.4 & 0.4 & 0.1 \\\\\n & Schema-informed & 5.8 & 0.3 & \\textbf{0.0} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{DocRED} & Unconstrained & 18.5 & 29.7 & 2.5 & 0.1 \\\\\n & Schema-informed & 12.8 & 18.4 & 0.2 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_per_relation_dr.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-relation DR violation rates on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, unconstrained). 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\\_type, role)$\\to$allowed\\_entity\\_types constraints.\nThe grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the WikiEvents.}\n\\label{app:wikievents-kappa}\n% Data source: artifacts/w1c_wikievents_kappa_results.md\nWikiEvents role-level tightness (with $|\\mathcal{E}|{=}27$) has mean $\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.82$ and the highest AC rate (50\\%), compared to 21\\% for medium-tightness ($0.80 \\leq \\kappa < 0.90$) and 30\\% for loose ($\\kappa < 0.80$) roles; this is consistent with the RE observation that show 0\\% AC despite $\\kappa{=}0.963$, while roles that require rare KAIROS-specific types (e.g., Crime$\\to$CRM, Sentence$\\to$SEN) show near-100\\% AC because LLMs default to common types (PER, ORG).\nThe loose group's elevated rate (30\\%) is driven by high-frequency roles such as Attack.Target (126 args, 34\\% AC, $\\kappa{=}0.59$), where systematic model errors outweigh the permissive     "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}); at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\\%), it becomes impractical.\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \\emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), four models (7--70B), and three tokenizer families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned; \\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); zero-shot evaluation (7--70B) reveals DR drops 42--99\\% across models and datasets, with 76--99\\% on SciERC, but total violations (DR+CF) decrease by $\\sim$31\\%, reflecting conversion that fine-tuning resolves (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\nWe make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item A comprehensive evaluation across 3 IE datasets, 4 models (7--70B), and 2 tasks: under fine-tuning, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an enabling dependency where on SciERC neither layer alone exceeds 24\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); event extraction on WikiEvents validates applicability beyond RE (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nViolations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.\nAt competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\\%), even 1--2\\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}); at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\\%), it becomes impractical.\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nA decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \\emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \\emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \\emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.\nOntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with preliminary 70B-class validation (\\S\\ref{sec:70b-results}).\nLoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% ($\\pm$0.8\\,pp, $+$10\\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on SciERC (1.54$\\times$ latency).\nDR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\% fine-tuned; \\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); zero-shot evaluation reveals DR drops 42--99\\% across models and datasets, with 76--99\\% on SciERC, but total violations (DR+CF) decrease by $\\sim$31\\%, reflecting conversion that fine-tuning resolves (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\nWe make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n    \\item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\\S\\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.\n    \\item A comprehensive evaluation across 3 IE datasets, 4 models (7--70B), and 2 tasks: under fine-tuning, DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\% at R-F1 43--46\\% with CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.4\\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an enabling dependency where on SciERC neither layer alone exceeds 24\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:scierc-results}); event extraction on WikiEvents validates applicability beyond RE (\\S\\ref{sec:wikievents}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
