    "originalFile": "% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)\n\n\\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}\n\\label{sec:runtime}\nS1 overhead is $+$13.1\\% on SciERC, lower on DocRED due to amortization over longer sequences; under LoRA, $+$10.0\\% with 37\\% lower absolute latency.\nConstraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}).\n\n\\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}\n\\label{sec:beam-results}\nWith LoRA, beam search ($k{=}4$)~\\cite{anderson2017guided} eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--46); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\\% on both datasets (CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\nTotal latency: 1.54$\\times$ (beam 1.32$\\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:beam-latency}).\n\n% exp_ids: r16_70b_scierc_zs_s1, r16_70b_docred_zs_s1, 70b_lora_scierc, 70b_lora_docred_200\n\\paragraph{70B evaluation.}\n\\label{sec:70b-results}\nOn Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (AWQ INT4), zero-shot S1 eliminates DR on both datasets: SciERC 6.41\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 11.24), DocRED 14.98\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 13.05); CF rises to 3.50--7.01\\%, comparable to 8B zero-shot CF.\n% exp_ids: eval_70b_lora_scierc_s1_constrained, eval_70b_lora_docred_200_s1_constrained\nLoRA fine-tuning confirms scale-independent composability: SciERC DR 1.50\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 44.53, $-$0.20\\,pp); DocRED (200-document subset; Appendix~\\ref{app:power-analysis}) DR 0.22\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% (R-F1 43.95, $+$0.15\\,pp); CF drops from 3.50\\% (zero-shot) to 0.18--1.33\\% (LoRA), consistent with the 8B pattern (9.90\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.18\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:composability}).\n\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same token-level constraints as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}---CFG-based JSON structure enforcement plus vocabulary membership masking---providing a direct head-to-head comparison: RV/ET$\\to$0\\% but only 3\\% DR reduction, vs.\\ S1's 94\\%, confirming that CFG enforcement alone is insufficient for type-valid IE (Appendix~\\ref{app:gcd-comparison}).\nWithout JSON structural enforcement, trie-only constraints yield 9.1--50.8\\% parse failure and R-F1 19.95--41.16 vs.\\ OntoCoDec's 43.77--45.47 (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}).\n",

    "oldString": "In code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).",
    "newString": "In code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata; PICARD prunes invalid beam hypotheses post-generation, whereas OntoCoDec applies proactive token-level masking before sampling, and maintains a dynamic entity registry across multi-relation outputs rather than single-output SQL queries (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\nWe provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.\nNeuroLogic Decoding~\\cite{lu2021neurologic} enforces predicate-logic constraints via beam-level reranking; OntoCoDec applies constraints per-token via forward masking, avoiding beam-width dependence.\nIn code generation, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\\S\\ref{sec:composition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}\nGoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.\nGoLLIE and KnowCoder embed schema knowledge via code-style prompts, representing training-time schema internalization without runtime enforcement; GoLLIE-7B eliminates DR via its dataclass format but at severe coverage cost (E-F1${=}$9.17, R-F1${=}$0.0, 46\\% empty output), while OntoCoDec with LoRA achieves R-F1${=}$45.47 at DR${=}$0.09\\%, surpassing KnowCoder-7B (supervised R-F1${=}$40.0)~\\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}\n\\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \\citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation but discards up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives.\nEnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} uses ASP; IterGen~\\cite{ugare2025itergen} backtracks on violations---OntoCoDec uses forward-only masking at $O(|V|)$ per step, avoiding backtracking latency.\nGrammar-only enforcement (GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only 3\\% DR reduction (\\S\\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}); DCCD~\\cite{dccd2026} explores draft-conditioned constrained decoding.\nExtraction tasks are robust to format constraints~\\cite{tam2024letmespeak} despite degradation in other domains~\\cite{banerjee2025crane,formattax2026}.\n",

```text
1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At competitive quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}); at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time constraints.
18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), three 8B-class models across three tokenizer families, with preliminary 70B-class validation (\S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
20	LoRA + greedy S1 achieves near-complete DR elimination: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% ($\pm$0.8\,pp, $+$10\% latency); beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violation types on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
21	DR enforcement converts ontologically invalid violations to individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% fine-tuned; \S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); zero-shot evaluation reveals DR drops 42--99\% across models and datasets, with 76--99\% on SciERC, but total violations (DR+CF) decrease by $\sim$31\%, reflecting conversion that fine-tuning resolves (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
22	
23	We make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:
24	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
25	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.
26	    \item A comprehensive evaluation across 3 IE datasets, 3 models (8B-class), and 2 tasks, with preliminary 70B validation: under fine-tuning, DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% at R-F1 43--46\% with CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\%; zero-shot ablation reveals an enabling dependency where on SciERC neither layer alone exceeds 24\% DR reduction (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); event extraction on WikiEvents validates applicability beyond RE (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

_source line 4405 · 2026-05-22T13:08:50.425Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017tj9jQmoUwooTjsoM3t9er`</summary>

```text
1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	where $\wedge$ denotes element-wise AND over the vocabulary $V$; a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the empirical enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property enforced only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation, with DR (ontologically invalid type pair) more severe than CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency).
17	
18	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
19	\label{eq:tightness}
20	We define per-relation constraint tightness $\kappa(r) = 1 - |\sigma(r)|/|\mathcal{E}|^2$ and the schema-level mean $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$.
21	Across three schemas---WikiEvents ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.029$), SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$)---DR reduction ranges from 94--99\% (low $\bar{\kappa}$) to 42--90\% (high $\bar{\kappa}$); entity type confusability is a critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study},~\ref{app:tcr}).
22	
23	\paragraph{Greedy limitation and fallback cascade.}
24	\label{sec:greedy-limitation}
25	The semantic layer commits entity types greedily---an instance of Level~3 (non-local) constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability})---so CF violations arise when the same entity receives different types across triples; beam search mitigates this in favorable configurations (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
26	Grammar-first fallback triggers on 0.5--5.7\% of type-bearing positions under zero-shot decoding, correlating with $\bar{\kappa}$; fine-tuning virtually eliminates fallback ($\leq$\,0.11\%), transitioning the semantic layer from primary enforcer to safety net.
27	Causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs exclusively at fallback positions (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Non-rectangularity and independent enforcement.}
30	\label{sec:rectangularity}
31	Type signatures are non-rectangular (0/7 SciERC, 23/96 DocRED rectangular); independent enforcement is a sound over-approximation admitting 33--37\% spurious pairs; fine-tuning eliminates all residual DR (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
32	
```

68	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
69	\clearpage
70	\section*{Limitations}
71	
72	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
73	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama; DR\,$\leq$\,1\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
74	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
75	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
76	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
77	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
78	On 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\%\,$\to$\,12.5\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\% parse failure in both settings.
79	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
80	Schema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.
81	Post-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.
82	
83	\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}
84	Zero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\% compared to $\sim$45\% for fine-tuned systems.
85	Zero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).
86	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
87	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.8\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
88	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
89	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
90	
91	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
92	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
93	Our approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.
94	Continuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.
95	This assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \texttt{Protein} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Complex} vs.\ \texttt{Protein\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.
96	70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.
97	Our evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the continuation masking mechanism may behave differently for languages without whitespace-delimited tokenization (e.g., Chinese, Japanese), which we leave to future work.
98	Fine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\%\,$\to$\,0\%; Appendix~\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.
99	
100	
101	% === Ethics Statement ===
102	\section*{Ethics Statement}
103	
104	This work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.
105	No human subjects were involved in the experiments.
106	The constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.
107	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

DR\\,$\\leq$\\,1\\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\\%\\,$\\to$\\,1.27\\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.\nCF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\\,pp). CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).\nGlobal entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.\nCF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.\nEntity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\\,pp for Qwen vs.\\ $-$0.64\\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\\% vs.\\ 6.8\\%).\nOn 70B DocRED LoRA, parse failure rises modestly under S1 (10.0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12.5\\%) due to generation timeouts on long documents rather than JSON format errors; SciERC (shorter documents) shows 0\\% parse failure in both settings.\nResidual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\\%), schema coverage gaps (33\\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).\nSchema coverage gaps (one-third of residual violations) arise because RE constraints are extracted from training-data type pairs, which may not cover all valid combinations; larger training sets or manual constraint table completion can reduce this gap.\nPost-hoc type consensus re-voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:tcr}) reduces CF violations by 18--92\\% depending on model and dataset, confirming that first-assignment ordering sensitivity is a primary CF source; however, integrating consensus into the decoding loop remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation coverage.}\nZero-shot results cover three 7--8B-class models, where relation F1 remains around 4--8\\% compared to $\\sim$45\\% for fine-tuned systems.\nZero-shot evaluation covers three 8B-class and one 70B-class model; fine-tuning composability is validated on 8B-class (two models, full datasets) and 70B-class models (one model; SciERC full, DocRED 200-document subset).\nScaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.\nComposability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.8\\,pp; EE: RoV$\\to$0\\%, AC$\\leq$0.8\\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\\%, Llama 0.46\\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.\nWikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).\nAll main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC (551 documents) and DocRED (998 documents) test sets across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling},~\\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}\nScalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\\,s; Appendix~\\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.\nOur approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.\nContinuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.\nThis assumption holds for our evaluation ontologies but may be challenged by ontologies with morphologically similar type names (e.g., \\texttt{Protein} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Complex} vs.\\ \\texttt{Protein\\_Domain}); when type names share a common prefix longer than one token, masking granularity depends on the tokenizer's subword segmentation.\n70B experiments use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may alter logit distributions at mask boundaries; the impact on constraint enforcement precision has not been isolated.\nOur evaluation is limited to English-language datasets; the continuation masking mechanism may behave differently for languages without whitespace-delimited tokenization (e.g., Chinese, Japanese), which we leave to future work.\nFine-tuning composability was validated exclusively with LoRA; full fine-tuning with an untuned learning rate underperforms LoRA (R-F1 37.89 vs.\\ 43.77 on SciERC) but confirms that constraints remain effective (DR 2.74\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:full-ft}); other fine-tuning methods may exhibit different constraint interaction patterns and warrant further investigation.\n\n\n% === Ethics Statement ===\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available benchmark datasets (SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents) with no personally identifiable information.\nNo human subjects were involved in the experiments.\nThe constrained decoding method operates on model outputs and does not introduce new privacy or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base language models.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\n\n% === Bibliography ===\n\\bibliography{relwork_new_entries}\n\n% === Appendix ===\n\\appendix\n\n\\input{appendix_prompts}\n\\input{appendix_related_work}\n\\input{appendix_discussion}\n\\input{appendix_llama_per_relation}\n\\input{appendix_sampling_table}\n\\input{appendix_beam_search}\n\\input{appendix_reproducibility}\n\\input{appendix_doc_length}\n\\input{appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\\input{appendix_case_studies}\n\\input{appendix_scalability}\n\\input{appendix_full_ft}\n\\input{appendix_tcr}\n\\input{appendix_kappa_case_study}\n\\input{appendix_pumping_lemma}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

1	% §5 Setup (compressed from Violation Landscape)

5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}), near-eliminating DR (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%) with minimal overhead; and LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}), achieving zero total violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency).
8	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe; the deployment-relevant evaluation is the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%).
9	
10	\paragraph{Setup.}
11	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
12	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
13	All constraints are defined from schema-level supervision, independently of the evaluation split.
14	The framework handles both sources without modification.
15	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
16	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).
17	Baseline DR: 15.37\% (SciERC), 18.46\% (DocRED); schema-informed prompting reduces DR by 62\% on SciERC but is per-relation unreliable (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
18	
```

71	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
72	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
73	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
74	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
75	S1 reduces DR from 15.37\% to 0.93\% ($\downarrow$94\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\,$\to$\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).
76	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\%, reaching 0\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
77	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 48\% of predictions rather than correcting them (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}).
78	Total type-violation burden (Tot.\,=\,DR+CF) decreases from 15.45\% to 10.83\% ($\downarrow$30\%), reflecting \emph{violation conversion}: DR enforcement replaces ontologically invalid triples with individually valid but type-inconsistent ones (CF 0.08\%\,$\to$\,9.90\%); under fine-tuning, Tot.\ drops to 1.18\% with CF\,$\leq$\,1.4\% (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
79

82	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
83	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
84	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
85	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.
86	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \emph{empirical enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
87

103	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
104	\label{sec:composability}
105	% exp_ids: bootstrap_ci_ft_composability.json
106	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}); adding S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% with no significant $\Delta$R-F1 after Bonferroni correction (min.\ $p{=}0.014$; 95\% CIs $\subseteq [-0.76, {+}0.22]$\,pp; removed triples are 97.6--100\% false positives).
107	CF drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}); beam ($k{=}4$) with LoRA eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 43.60, 46.18; \S\ref{sec:beam-results}).

151	\label{sec:downstream-impact}
152	Among constraint-removed triples, only 0.49\% match gold labels (``lucky violations''; Appendix~\ref{app:lucky-violations}), and error analysis of 514 false negatives confirms only one (0.2\%) was blocked by constraints---the remaining 99.8\% reflect model limitations (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).

55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	RV and ET reach $\leq$0.03\% for all models on both datasets.
57	DR drops 76--99\% on SciERC and 42--90\% on DocRED; R-F1 improves in 5/6 settings.
58	CF direction depends on calibration: S1 introduces CF for calibrated models (up to 12.02\%; \S\ref{sec:discussion}) but reduces it for Mistral (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%); fine-tuning resolves both (\S\ref{sec:composability}).

61	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
62	S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).\footnote{Residual 0.03\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case (chimeric subword tokens across enum entries); reported upstream.}
63	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
64	CF increases from 0.10\% to 2.57\%, consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
65	Residual zero-shot DR (10.21\%) traces to grammar-first fallback under tight constraints ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$; \S\ref{sec:greedy-limitation}); causal tracing confirms all residual DR occurs at fallback positions (Appendix~\ref{app:causal-tracing}).
66	
67	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only, phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
68	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
69	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET but \emph{increases} DR to 26.17\%: vocabulary restriction makes OOV outputs evaluable, exposing latent type incompatibilities.
70	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV or ET, the same empirical enabling dependency observed on SciERC.
71	Residual DR (10.21\% vs.\ 0.93\% on SciERC) reflects high $\bar{\kappa}$ (0.842) and \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X ambiguity; fine-tuning narrows DR to $\leq$\,0.09\% (Table~\ref{tab:composability}; residual decomposition in Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).
72
